Wasserstein Projection Tests: Higher-Order Asymptotics and Connections to Empirical Likelihood
Sirui Lin, José Blanchet, Peter Glynn, Viet Anh Nguyen

Abstract
Tests of moment restrictions can be constructed by projecting the empirical distribution onto the set of laws satisfying the null. Wasserstein projection (WP) performs this projection by transporting mass in the sample space, rather than only reweighting observed atoms as in empirical likelihood (EL), and therefore incorporates both the ground geometry and the local variation of the moment function. Existing WP theory provides a first-order null limit but does not quantify finite-sample calibration or distinguish tests with the same limiting local power. We derive stochastic expansions of the rescaled WP statistic under the null and under Pitman-type local alternatives, identifying its order- n − 1 / 2 n^{-1/2} correction with a uniform remainder of order O ~ p ​ ( n − 1 ) \widetilde{O}_{p}(n^{-1}) . The resulting Edgeworth theory gives an order- O ~ ​ ( n − 1 ) \widetilde{O}(n^{-1}) size error for the plug-in WP test and an explicit order- n − 1 / 2 n^{-1/2} correction to local power. For scalar moments under local location shifts, WP, EL, and Hotelling’s T 2 T^{2} have the same first-order power, whereas their second-order ranking is determined by derivative-based curvature for WP and value-based skewness for EL. Extending the expansion by one further order, we construct two Bartlett-type corrections that improve coverage accuracy to O ~ ( n − 3 / 2 ) \widetilde{O}(n^{-3/2}) . Finally, we develop a certified localized dual algorithm that controls numerical error in the test decision. A theory-guided smooth fairness experiment using a common asymptotic chi-square reference quantile finds WP power gains over the corresponding EL and T 2 T^{2} tests in the derivative-curvature regime predicted by the comparison theory.



Keywords:
Optimal transport; Wasserstein projection; empirical likelihood; Edgeworth expansion; local power; Bartlett correction.

MSC 2020:
Primary 62G10, 62E20; secondary 62G20.

1
Introduction

Moment restrictions underlie estimating-equation methods
[
32
]
and empirical likelihood (EL) inference
[
44
,
45
,
34
]
. Given an empirical distribution
\mathbb{Q}_{n}
and a null model

\mathcal{P}_{0}=\{\mathbb{P}:\mathds{E}_{\mathbb{P}}[\mathbf{h}(X)]=\mathbf{0}\},

EL measures the discrepancy from
\mathbb{Q}_{n}
to
\mathcal{P}_{0}
by reweighting the observed sample. Wasserstein projection (WP) provides a geometric alternative:

R_{n}(\mathbf{h})=\inf_{\mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{P}_{0}}\mathds{W}_{c}(\mathbb{Q}_{n},\mathbb{P}),

the least transportation cost required to bring the empirical distribution into agreement with the null moment restriction. The associated test rejects for large values of
nR_{n}(\mathbf{h})
. This paper uses higher-order asymptotics to quantify finite-sample size distortion and to identify when transport geometry yields power gains.

WP is built from optimal transport (OT), which compares distributions through a ground cost on the sample space. Although estimation of an unrestricted Wasserstein distance suffers from the curse of dimensionality
[
24
,
26
,
58
]
, projection onto a model defined by finitely many moment restrictions retains a parametric rate
[
7
]
; related results extend to certain infinite-dimensional restrictions
[
50
]
. This distinction has made WP useful in hypothesis testing, fairness, and distributionally robust optimization. Other approaches, including Sinkhorn, smoothed, and sliced Wasserstein distances, address dimensionality by modifying or reducing the transport geometry
[
20
,
28
,
43
,
29
,
9
,
59
]
; our focus is instead the inferential behavior of the projection statistic.

The close connection between WP and EL makes their comparison especially informative. Both project the empirical law onto the same moment model, but they use different discrepancies. EL changes only the weights assigned to observed atoms, whereas WP can split and transport mass to new locations. WP therefore incorporates the ground geometry and the local variation of
\mathbf{h}
; it also remains finite whenever the convex hull of the range of
\mathbf{h}
contains the origin, including settings in which the EL constraint can be infeasible without adjustment
[
13
,
42
]
. At higher order, this structural difference becomes explicit: the WP power correction is governed by derivative-based curvature of the moment function, while the EL correction is governed by value-based moments of
h(X)
.

Existing WP theory provides its first-order null distribution and hence an asymptotically valid test
[
7
]
. First-order theory, however, does not quantify the error from approximating the finite-sample law by this limit. Nor can first-order theory distinguish WP from its main competitors under Pitman alternatives when the moment dimension is one (
d_{h}=1
), because WP, EL, and Hotelling’s
T^{2}
have the same limiting local-power function. Determining whether transport geometry helps therefore requires the next term in the size and power expansions. This is the central motivation for the higher-order analysis developed here.

Fairness testing gives a concrete example. Moment restrictions can encode equality of opportunity or equality of odds, and
R_{n}(\mathbf{h})
measures the smallest modification of the empirical distribution that makes a fixed classifier satisfy the chosen criterion
[
55
,
51
]
. The projected law also has a counterfactual interpretation as the least costly fair modification of the observed population. For nonlinear fairness restrictions the WP statistic generally has no closed form, so an implementable test additionally requires a reliable numerical procedure. Section
4
develops such a procedure and illustrates it in this setting.

More broadly, WP quantiles calibrate Wasserstein ambiguity radii and confidence regions in distributionally robust optimization
[
7
,
8
]
, while related projections quantify model sensitivity and stability
[
6
,
37
]
. In these applications, replacing an unavailable finite-sample quantile by its limiting approximation can produce systematic coverage or conservatism errors. Thus the higher-order expansion and Bartlett-type corrections below also improve uncertainty calibration beyond hypothesis testing; related higher-order questions for DRO are studied in
[
33
]
.

Throughout,
\widetilde{O}(\cdot)
and
\widetilde{O}_{p}(\cdot)
denote deterministic and
stochastic orders, respectively, with logarithmic factors in
n
suppressed.

Our contributions are as follows.

(1)

We derive stochastic expansions of
nR_{n}(\mathbf{h})
under the null and under Pitman-type local alternatives. The expansion identifies the leading quadratic term, its order-
n^{-1/2}
cubic correction, and a uniform remainder of order
\widetilde{O}(n^{-1})
; see Theorems
3.1
and
3.3
.

(2)

We translate the statistic expansion into inferential guarantees. The resulting Edgeworth theory yields an order-
\widetilde{O}(n^{-1})
size error for the plug-in WP test and an explicit order-
n^{-1/2}
correction to its local power; see Theorems
3.2
,
3.4
, and
3.5
.

(3)

For scalar moments under local location shifts, we compare WP with EL and Hotelling’s
T^{2}
. The tests have the same first-order power, while their second-order ranking is determined by a derivative-based WP curvature coefficient and a value-based EL skewness coefficient; see Proposition
3.8
, Table
1
, and Supplementary Sections S2.9.4 and S4.2.

(4)

We extend the statistic expansion by one further order and construct two Bartlett-type corrections for scalar moment restrictions. These corrections remove the leading order-
n^{-1}
calibration error and improve coverage accuracy to
\widetilde{O}(n^{-3/2})
; see Theorems
3.10
–
3.11
and Propositions
3.12
–
3.13
.

(5)

We develop a certified localized dual algorithm for WP testing when the projection lacks a closed form. The procedure controls the numerical error in the test decision and is illustrated through a nonlinear fairness experiment; see Theorem
4.3
, Algorithm 1 in Supplementary Section S2.10.4, and Section
4.2
.

Section
2
formulates the test, records the first-order benchmark, and summarizes the regularity conditions. Section
3
develops the higher-order expansions and their testing implications. Section
4
treats computation, and Section
5
discusses the WP–EL connection and broader extensions. The notation appendix and Supplementary Sections S2–S4 provide a comprehensive notation reference, all technical proofs, and computational checks.

2
Testing framework, first-order benchmark, and assumptions

We first fix the transport notation used throughout the paper.

Definition 2.1

(Optimal transport cost)
.

For a lower semicontinuous ground cost

c:\mathds{R}^{d_{X}}\times\mathds{R}^{d_{X}}\to[0,\infty]
and probability laws

\mathbb{Q},\mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathds{R}^{d_{X}})
, define

\displaystyle\mathds{W}_{c}(\mathbb{Q},\mathbb{P})\triangleq\inf_{\pi}\left\{\mathds{E}_{\pi}[c(\bar{X},X)]:\pi|_{\bar{X}}=\mathbb{Q},\ \pi|_{X}=\mathbb{P}\right\},

(1)

where the infimum ranges over probability laws on

\mathds{R}^{d_{X}}\times\mathds{R}^{d_{X}}
and
\pi|_{\bar{X}},\pi|_{X}
denote their marginals.

Throughout, we use the squared Mahalanobis ground cost

\displaystyle c(\bar{x},x)=\left\|\bar{x}-x\right\|_{\Sigma}^{2}\triangleq(\bar{x}-x)^{\top}\Sigma^{-1}(\bar{x}-x),\qquad\Sigma\in\mathbb{S}_{++}.

(2)

Mathematically,
\Sigma
specifies the local transport geometry.
In practice, it may encode scientifically meaningful relative perturbation costs, with covariance-based choices measuring transport in standardized, correlation-adjusted units and
\Sigma=I_{d_{X}}
recovering the ordinary squared Euclidean cost.
We use “distance” for

\mathds{W}_{c}
even though the squared cost does not itself satisfy the triangle
inequality.

Let
X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}
be i.i.d. observations in
\mathds{R}^{d_{X}}
with common law

\mathbb{P}^{\star}
, and let
\mathbf{h}:\mathds{R}^{d_{X}}\to\mathds{R}^{d_{h}}
be a user-specified moment
function. We test whether the data-generating distribution satisfies the moment
restriction

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{0}:\mathds{E}_{\mathbb{P}^{\star}}[\mathbf{h}(X)]=\mathbf{0}\qquad\text{vs.}\qquad\mathcal{H}_{1}:\mathds{E}_{\mathbb{P}^{\star}}[\mathbf{h}(X)]\neq\mathbf{0}.

(3)

Equivalently, if

\mathcal{P}_{0}\triangleq\{\mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathds{R}^{d_{X}}):\mathds{E}_{\mathbb{P}}[\mathbf{h}(X)]=\mathbf{0}\},

then
\mathcal{H}_{0}
is the membership statement
\mathbb{P}^{\star}\in\mathcal{P}_{0}
. This setup
includes ordinary moment tests, inference based on estimating equations

\mathbf{h}(x)=\mathbf{f}(\theta,x)
, and fairness tests in which the moment restriction
encodes the target fairness criterion
[
55
]
.

Let
\mathbb{Q}_{n}=n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\delta_{X_{i}}
denote the empirical law.

Definition 2.2

(Wasserstein projection)
.

The WP distance from
\mathbb{Q}_{n}
to the null model is

\displaystyle R_{n}(\mathbf{h})\triangleq\inf_{\mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{P}_{0}}\mathds{W}_{c}(\mathbb{Q}_{n},\mathbb{P})=\inf_{\mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathds{R}^{d_{X}})}\left\{\mathds{W}_{c}(\mathbb{Q}_{n},\mathbb{P}):\mathds{E}_{\mathbb{P}}[\mathbf{h}(X)]=\mathbf{0}\right\}.

(4)

This is a projection test: the null determines a set of distributions, and the
statistic measures the distance from the empirical law to that set. Hence

R_{n}(\mathbf{h})
is the least cost required to transport the data into agreement
with the null, and a large value is evidence against
\mathcal{H}_{0}
. Under the
null, the empirical moment fluctuates at order
n^{-1/2}
and the transport
cost is locally quadratic, so
R_{n}(\mathbf{h})=O_{p}(n^{-1})
and
nR_{n}(\mathbf{h})
has a
nondegenerate limit.

For nominal level
\varrho
, the WP test rejects when

\displaystyle\text{reject }\mathcal{H}_{0}\quad\text{if}\quad nR_{n}(\mathbf{h})>\hat{z}_{1-\varrho},

(5)

where
\hat{z}_{1-\varrho}
is the plug-in
(1-\varrho)
-quantile of the
limiting null law. Hotelling’s
T^{2}
, empirical likelihood (EL), and WP all
measure violation of the same moment restriction but impose different
geometries. Hotelling’s statistic measures the empirical moment directly; EL
projects by reweighting the observed atoms; WP transports mass in the sample
space and therefore responds to both the ground metric
\Sigma
and local
changes in
\mathbf{h}
. Their comparison isolates whether this transport geometry
improves finite-sample calibration or power after their common first-order
behavior is taken into account.

2.1
First-order benchmark

Near the null, the transport perturbations required to satisfy the moment restriction are
small, so linearizing the moment restriction reduces the WP problem to a quadratic program
that determines the test’s first-order approximation. The exact rescaled dual representation is given in Supplementary Section S2.1.1. We use

[d]\triangleq\{1,\ldots,d\}
and let
\mathcal{D}\mathbf{h}(x)\in\mathds{R}^{d_{h}\times d_{X}}
denote the
Jacobian of
\mathbf{h}
, with
\mathcal{D}h^{\beta}(x)
its
\beta
th row. For a vector
u
,

u^{\otimes k}
denotes its
k
th outer power, and

\left\langle A,u^{\otimes k}\right\rangle
denotes full contraction with a
k
th-order tensor

A
. Define

\displaystyle H_{n}

\displaystyle\triangleq\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{h}(X_{i}),

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}_{n}

\displaystyle\triangleq\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathcal{D}\mathbf{h}(X_{i})\Sigma\mathcal{D}\mathbf{h}(X_{i})^{\top}.

Expanding the inner dual objective then gives the compact approximation

nR_{n}(\mathbf{h})=H_{n}^{\top}\mathcal{V}_{n}^{-1}H_{n}+o_{p}(1).

This yields the standard first-order null law below; see

[
7
, Propositions 3 and 5]
.

Proposition 2.3

(First-order null limit)
.

Suppose that
\mathbf{h}\in C^{1}(\mathds{R}^{d_{X}})
is bounded, the moment constraint is
feasible, and the following two matrices are positive definite:

W\triangleq\mathds{E}[\mathbf{h}(X)^{\otimes 2}],\qquad V\triangleq\mathds{E}[\mathcal{D}\mathbf{h}(X)\Sigma\mathcal{D}\mathbf{h}(X)^{\top}].

Then, under
\mathcal{H}_{0}
,

nR_{n}(\mathbf{h})\overset{d}{\longrightarrow}H^{\top}V^{-1}H,\qquad H\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},W).

Boundedness is used here only as a convenient sufficient condition for this
first-order benchmark. The higher-order results below instead accommodate
unbounded moment functions through the stated derivative-envelope and moment
conditions.

The limit justifies the plug-in critical value in (
5
),
but it leaves its finite-sample accuracy unresolved. Both the statistic and
its critical value contain estimated quantities, so asymptotic validity alone
does not identify the leading size distortion or indicate how it should be
corrected. This requires a distributional expansion beyond the quadratic
limit.

First-order theory also cannot determine when transport geometry improves
power. For scalar moments under Pitman location shifts, WP, EL, and Hotelling’s

T^{2}
have the same limiting local power; their first meaningful ranking appears
in the order-
n^{-1/2}
term. That term distinguishes the derivative-based
geometry used by WP from the value-based information used by EL. Extending the
expansion by one further order then identifies the leading calibration error and
makes a Bartlett-type correction possible. These three gaps motivate the null,
local-power, and higher-order developments in Section
3
.

2.2
Assumptions

We state the regularity conditions here to make the scope of the main results
explicit. Assumptions
2.4
–
2.9
support the
stochastic expansion of the WP statistic, while Assumption
2.10

provides the additional moment control needed for its Edgeworth expansion.
Further motivation and representative bounds are collected in
Supplementary Section S2.1.2.

2.2.1
Conditions on the moment function
\mathbf{h}

Assumption 2.4

(Feasibility of the moment constraint)
.

The origin lies in the interior of the convex hull of the range of
\mathbf{h}
:

\mathbf{0}\in\inte\left(\conv\{\mathbf{h}(x):x\in\mathds{R}^{d_{X}}\}\right).

This interiority requirement is a Slater-type condition ensuring that the WP projection
admits an attained finite-dimensional dual representation; see Supplementary Section S2.7.2.
Expanding that dual formulation is the central device driving our higher-order theory.

Assumption 2.5

(Lipschitz control of the Jacobian)
.

We assume
\mathbf{h}\in C^{3}(\mathds{R}^{d_{X}})
. There exists a function
\kappa_{1}:\mathds{R}^{d_{X}}\to\mathds{R}_{+}
such that, for all
(x,\Delta,\zeta)\in\mathds{R}^{d_{X}}\times\mathds{R}^{d_{X}}\times\mathds{R}^{d_{h}}
,

\Big\|\sum_{\beta=1}^{d_{h}}\zeta^{(\beta)}\left(\mathcal{D}h^{\beta}(x+\Delta)-\mathcal{D}h^{\beta}(x)\right)\Big\|_{2}\leq\kappa_{1}(x)\left\|\Delta\right\|_{2}\left\|\zeta\right\|_{2},

where
\mathbf{h}=(h^{\beta})_{\beta\in[d_{h}]}
and
\zeta=(\zeta^{(\beta)})_{\beta\in[d_{h}]}
.

In particular,
\kappa_{1}
bounds directional Hessians and enforces growth compatible with the
squared ground cost, preventing projection degeneracy; a counterexample and alternative cost
choices are given in Supplementary Section S2.1.2.

Assumption 2.6

(Local Lipschitz control of the Hessian)
.

There exist
\kappa_{2}:\mathds{R}^{d_{X}}\to\mathds{R}_{+}
and
\hat{\delta}>0
such that, for all
(x,\Delta,\zeta)\in\mathds{R}^{d_{X}}\times\mathds{R}^{d_{X}}\times\mathds{R}^{d_{h}}
with
\left\|\Delta\right\|_{2}\leq\hat{\delta}
,

\Big\|\sum_{\beta=1}^{d_{h}}\zeta^{(\beta)}\left(\mathcal{D}^{2}h^{\beta}(x+\Delta)-\mathcal{D}^{2}h^{\beta}(x)\right)\Big\|_{2}\leq\kappa_{2}(x)\left\|\Delta\right\|_{2}\left\|\zeta\right\|_{2}.

This condition controls the third-order Taylor remainder; without such smoothness, the
projection may enter a different boundary-layer regime, as discussed in Supplementary Section S2.1.2.

2.2.2
Conditions on the data-generating distribution
\mathbb{P}^{\star}

Assumption 2.7

(Absolute continuity and Cramér regularity)
.

The distribution
\mathbb{P}^{\star}
has a density with respect to Lebesgue
measure on
\mathds{R}^{d_{X}}
. Moreover, after deterministic affine redundancies
have been removed, the functions of a single observation
X
whose sample
averages enter the Edgeworth and signed-root expansions jointly satisfy
the standard multivariate nonlattice (Cramér) regularity condition. The
precise collection of functions and the formal condition are given in
Supplementary Section S2.6.

The nonlattice requirement is used only to justify the Edgeworth expansions.

Assumption 2.8

(Null hypothesis and nondegeneracy)
.

Under
\mathbb{P}^{\star}
,

\mathds{E}[\mathbf{h}(X)]=\mathbf{0},\qquad\mathrm{Cov}(\mathbf{h}(X),\mathbf{h}(X))\in\mathbb{S}_{++},\qquad\mathds{E}\left[\mathcal{D}\mathbf{h}(X)\Sigma\mathcal{D}\mathbf{h}(X)^{\top}\right]\in\mathbb{S}_{++}.

The first condition is the null hypothesis. The two positive-definiteness
conditions rule out degenerate moment variation and a degenerate local
transport metric, respectively.

Assumption 2.9

(Moment envelope for the asymptotic expansion)
.

Under
\mathbb{P}^{\star}
,

\mathds{E}\left[\left(\left\|\mathbf{h}(X)\right\|_{2}+\left\|\mathcal{D}\mathbf{h}(X)\right\|_{2}+\kappa_{1}(X)+\kappa_{2}(X)\right)^{8}\right]<\infty.

These moments control the stochastic Taylor remainder uniformly with probability
at least
1-O(n^{-1})
.

Assumption 2.10

(Moment condition for the Edgeworth expansion)
.

Under
\mathbb{P}^{\star}
,

\mathds{E}\left[\left\|\mathcal{D}\mathbf{h}(X)\right\|_{2}^{8}\kappa_{1}(X)^{4}\right]<\infty.

This product moment supplies the fourth-moment bound for the summands entering

\mathcal{K}_{n}
, as required for an order-
n^{-1}
Edgeworth expansion.

3
Main results

3.1
Second-order asymptotic expansion

We first derive an expansion of the WP statistic in powers of
n^{-\frac{1}{2}}
and use it to obtain a second-order Edgeworth approximation based on classical normal-approximation theory
[
5
]
.
In this section, sample-level probabilities are evaluated under the product
law
(\mathbb{P}^{\star})^{\otimes n}
, and
\mathds{E}
denotes expectation under that law;
for a single observation,
\mathbb{P}^{\star}
retains its usual meaning.

Theorem 3.1

(Second-order asymptotic expansion)
.

If Assumptions
2.4
–
2.9
hold, then there is a deterministic sequence
\delta_{n}=\widetilde{O}\left(n^{-1}\right)
and an integer
N
such that when
n\geq N
, we have

\displaystyle nR_{n}(\mathbf{h})=\left\langle\mathcal{V}_{n},\xi_{n}^{\otimes 2}\right\rangle+\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\left\langle\mathcal{K}_{n},\xi_{n}^{\otimes 3}\right\rangle+\varepsilon_{n}.

(6)

The remainder satisfies
\mathbb{P}^{\star}\left(\left|\varepsilon_{n}\right|\leq\delta_{n}\right)=1-O(n^{-1})
.
Here
\mathcal{V}_{n}
,
\xi_{n}
, and
\mathcal{K}_{n}
are empirical quantities dependent on
\mathbf{h}
via

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}_{n}~

\displaystyle\triangleq~\left(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathcal{D}h^{\beta}(X_{i})\Sigma\mathcal{D}h^{\gamma}(X_{i})^{\top}\right)_{\beta,\gamma\in[d_{h}]}\in\mathds{R}^{d_{h}\times d_{h}},

\displaystyle\xi_{n}~

\displaystyle\triangleq~\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathcal{V}_{n}^{-1}\mathbf{h}(X_{i})\triangleq\left(\xi_{n}^{(\beta)}\right)_{\beta\in[d_{h}]}\in\mathds{R}^{d_{h}},

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}_{n}~

\displaystyle\triangleq\left(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathcal{D}h^{\beta}(X_{i})\Sigma\mathcal{D}^{2}h^{\gamma}(X_{i})\Sigma\mathcal{D}h^{\omega}(X_{i})^{\top}\right)_{\beta,\gamma,\omega\in[d_{h}]}\in(\mathds{R}^{d_{h}})^{\otimes 3}.

The proof, the explicit choice of
N
, and exact checks for linear and quadratic moments are
given, respectively, in Supplementary Sections S2.7, S2.12, and S2.5. The latter checks
verify the expansion against exact formulas for linear and quadratic moment functions.

To pass from Theorem
3.1
to a distributional approximation, its leading
terms can be represented as the squared norm of a signed-root statistic. Under the null,
the order-
n^{-1/2}
Edgeworth correction is odd and therefore integrates to zero over the
symmetric acceptance region. The signed-root construction, its moment expansion, and this
cancellation are given in Supplementary Sections S2.8.1–S2.8.2 and S2.13.

We next establish a valid Edgeworth expansion under the regularity assumptions imposed in Section
2.2
.

Theorem 3.2

(Second-order Edgeworth expansion)
.

Under Assumptions
2.4
–
2.10
, for every fixed
z_{0}>0
,

\displaystyle\sup_{z\geq z_{0}}\left|\mathbb{P}^{\star}\left(nR_{n}(\mathbf{h})\leq z\right)-\Psi(z)\right|=\widetilde{O}\left(\frac{1}{n}\right),

(7)

where
\Psi
is the CDF of the random variable
H^{\top}V^{-1}H
with
H\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},W)
. In particular, the expansion holds at every fixed
z>0
.

The cancellation of the
n^{-\frac{1}{2}}
term under the null is common for chi-squared-like
statistics, including likelihood-ratio
[
10
, Equation (30)]
and empirical-likelihood
statistics
[
30
, Section 3.2]
. Under local alternatives, this term is
generally nonzero and determines the second-order power correction derived in
Theorem
3.5
.

3.2
Asymptotic expansion under local alternatives

We now let the one-observation law
\mathbb{P}_{n}^{\star}
vary with
n
, draw

X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}
independently from this law, and write
\mathds{E}_{n}
for the
corresponding expectation. For sample-level events,
\mathbb{P}_{n}^{\star}
denotes the
product law
(\mathbb{P}_{n}^{\star})^{\otimes n}
. We consider Pitman-type local alternatives satisfying

\mathds{E}_{n}[\mathbf{h}(X)]=O(n^{-1/2}),

so the violation of the null moment condition is of the same order as sampling
noise. The uniform nondegeneracy, moment, and Cramér-type conditions needed
for the expansion are stated and explained in
Supplementary Section S2.8.3. Their role is to keep the
stochastic expansion and its remainder uniform along the sequence

(\mathbb{P}_{n}^{\star})_{n\geq 1}
.

Theorem 3.3

(Asymptotic expansion under local alternatives)
.

Suppose Assumptions
2.4
–
2.6
and the uniform
local-alternative conditions in Assumption S2.23 of
Supplementary Section S2.8.3 hold. Then there is a
deterministic sequence
\delta_{n}=\widetilde{O}(n^{-1})
and an integer
N
such that, for

n\geq N
,

\displaystyle nR_{n}(\mathbf{h})=\left\langle\mathcal{V}_{n},\xi_{n}^{\otimes 2}\right\rangle+\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\left\langle\mathcal{K}_{n},\xi_{n}^{\otimes 3}\right\rangle+\varepsilon_{n}.

Here
\xi_{n}
,
\mathcal{V}_{n}
, and
\mathcal{K}_{n}
are defined immediately after
(
6
) in Theorem
3.1
,
and
\mathbb{P}_{n}^{\star}\left(\left|\varepsilon_{n}\right|\leq\delta_{n}\right)=1-O(n^{-1})
.

The proof follows the null expansion with uniform bounds in place of fixed-law
bounds and is given in Supplementary Section S2.8.3. A stronger set
of conditions, needed only for the Edgeworth power expansion, is stated beside
that proof in Supplementary Section S2.9.3.

3.3
Implications for WP-based hypothesis testing

For confidence level
1-\varrho\in(0,1)
, the ideal asymptotic rule rejects

\mathcal{H}_{0}
when
nR_{n}(\mathbf{h})>z_{1-\varrho}
, where
z_{1-\varrho}
is the

(1-\varrho)
-quantile of the limiting law of
nR_{n}(\mathbf{h})
. Under suitable
assumptions, this law is that of the quadratic form
H^{\top}V^{-1}H
, with

H\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},W)
. Because the population matrices
W
and
V
are
unknown, however, this quantile is not directly available.
To address this issue, we use the sample analogues

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}_{n}\triangleq\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{h}(X_{i})^{\otimes 2},\quad\mathcal{V}_{n}\triangleq\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathcal{D}\mathbf{h}(X_{i})\Sigma\mathcal{D}\mathbf{h}(X_{i})^{\top},

(8)

and consider the plug-in CDF
\hat{F}_{n}
defined by

\displaystyle\hat{F}_{n}(z)\triangleq\int_{\left\{v\in\mathds{R}^{d_{h}}:~v^{\top}\mathcal{W}_{n}^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathcal{V}_{n}^{-1}\mathcal{W}_{n}^{\frac{1}{2}}v\leq z\right\}}\phi(v)\mathrm{d}v,

where
\phi
is the density function of the standard normal distribution on
\mathds{R}^{d_{h}}
. By the strong law of large numbers and Assumption
2.8
,
\mathcal{W}_{n}
and
\mathcal{V}_{n}
are eventually invertible almost surely. Thus
\mathcal{W}_{n}^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathcal{V}_{n}^{-1}\mathcal{W}_{n}^{\frac{1}{2}}
and the following plug-in quantile are well defined on an event whose probability tends to one.

We therefore estimate
z_{1-\varrho}
by the
(1-\varrho)
-quantile of
\hat{F}_{n}
:

\hat{z}_{1-\varrho}\triangleq\hat{F}_{n}^{-1}(1-\varrho).

(9)

The WP decision rule is therefore

reject
\mathcal{H}_{0}
if
nR_{n}(\mathbf{h})>\hat{z}_{1-\varrho}
.

(WP test)

By Slutsky’s theorem, under the null the acceptance probability of the decision
rule in (
WP test
) converges to
1-\varrho
, or equivalently its Type-I
error converges to
\varrho
. This is the desired first-order property.

3.3.1
Confidence level expansion

The confidence level of the WP-based test is quantified by

\underbrace{\mathbb{P}^{\star}\left(nR_{n}(\mathbf{h})\leq\hat{z}_{1-\varrho}\right)}_{\text{Actual confidence level}}~~=~~\underbrace{1-\varrho}_{\text{Desired confidence level}}~~+~~\text{error}.

The error term characterizes the order at which the actual confidence level
converges to the target. This error has two sources: (i) the finite-sample

(1-\varrho)
-quantile of
nR_{n}(\mathbf{h})
is unavailable, so we use the quantile
of its
limiting
distribution; and (ii) this limiting distribution
depends on unknown population quantities that must be estimated. To quantify
the error, we need the second-order expansion of
nR_{n}(\mathbf{h})
from
Theorem
3.1
and the corresponding expansion of the plug-in
quantile
\hat{z}_{1-\varrho}
derived below.
The statistic expansion and the corresponding expansion of the plug-in critical value can be
combined through a signed-root reduction. The quantile expansion and this reduction are
given with the proof in Supplementary Sections S2.9.1–S2.9.2.

Theorem 3.4

(Coverage accuracy of the WP test I)
.

Under Assumptions
2.4
–
2.10
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}^{\star}\left(nR_{n}(\mathbf{h})\leq\hat{z}_{1-\varrho}\right)=1-\varrho+\widetilde{O}\left(\frac{1}{n}\right).

(10)

According to
[
57
, Section 23.3]
, the above result shows that the confidence level of (
WP test
) is accurate up to order
\widetilde{O}(n^{-1})
. The rate
n^{-1}
is typical for the coverage error of both parametric likelihood and nonparametric methods (see, e.g., the discussions of empirical likelihood in
[
44
, Section 2.6]
and the bootstrap in
[
31
, Section 3.5]
).

When
\mathbf{h}(x)=\mathbf{f}(\theta,x)
, inversion of the WP test gives the confidence region

\hat{\Theta}_{n}=\{\theta:nR_{n}(\mathbf{f}(\theta,\cdot))\leq\hat{z}_{1-\varrho}(\theta)\},

(11)

where
\hat{z}_{1-\varrho}(\theta)
is the plug-in quantile computed using the
moment function
\mathbf{f}(\theta,\cdot)
. For each fixed true value
\theta_{0}

for which the assumptions of Theorem
3.4

hold with
\mathbf{h}(x)=\mathbf{f}(\theta_{0},x)
, its pointwise coverage satisfies

\mathbb{P}^{\star}(\theta_{0}\in\hat{\Theta}_{n})=1-\varrho+\widetilde{O}(n^{-1})
.

3.3.2
Local power expansion

At confidence level
1-\varrho
, the power is

\mathbb{P}_{n}^{\star}(nR_{n}(\mathbf{h})>\hat{z}_{1-\varrho})
. We expand this probability under
the Pitman-type alternatives introduced above. The first-order term gives the
limiting power, while the
n^{-1/2}
term distinguishes tests that share that
limit. The latter requires the stronger uniform moment and convergence
conditions in Supplementary Section S2.9.3; unlike classical
contiguity assumptions, these conditions are formulated for a sequence of
individual-observation distributions converging in total variation.

The following result gives the power of the WP test (
WP test
), namely, the probability that
nR_{n}(\mathbf{h})>\hat{z}_{1-\varrho}
. Recall that
\mathds{E}_{n}
denotes expectation under
\mathbb{P}_{n}^{\star}
. Define
W_{n}=\mathds{E}_{n}[\mathcal{W}_{n}]
and
V_{n}=\mathds{E}_{n}[\mathcal{V}_{n}]
, so that

\left(W_{n}\right)_{\beta\gamma}=\mathds{E}_{n}\left[h^{\beta}(X)h^{\gamma}(X)\right],~~\left(V_{n}\right)_{\beta\gamma}=\mathds{E}_{n}\left[\mathcal{D}h^{\beta}(X)\Sigma\mathcal{D}h^{\gamma}(X)^{\top}\right]~~\forall\beta,\gamma\in[d_{h}].

Theorem 3.5

(Power expansion of WP-based test)
.

Under Assumptions
2.4
–
2.6
and the additional Edgeworth conditions stated in Supplementary Section S2.9.3, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{n}^{\star}\left(nR_{n}(\mathbf{h})>\hat{z}_{1-\varrho}\right)={}

\displaystyle\underbrace{\int_{\left\|v+\tau_{n}\right\|_{2}^{2}>z_{n,1-\varrho}}\bar{\phi}(v)\mathrm{d}v}_{\text{first-order}}

\displaystyle{}+\underbrace{\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\int_{\left\|v+\tau_{n}\right\|_{2}^{2}>z_{n,1-\varrho}}\bar{p}(v)\bar{\phi}(v)\mathrm{d}v}_{\text{second-order}}+\widetilde{O}\left(\frac{1}{n}\right),

where
\tau_{n}=\sqrt{n}V_{n}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\mathds{E}_{n}\left[\mathbf{h}(X)\right]\in\mathds{R}^{d_{h}}
,
\bar{\phi}
is the density of
H\sim\mathcal{N}\big(0,V_{n}^{-\frac{1}{2}}W_{n}V_{n}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\big)
, and
z_{n,1-\varrho}
is the
(1-\varrho)
-quantile of
\left\|H\right\|_{2}^{2}
. The explicit correction polynomial
\bar{p}
, including the cumulant maps and Hermite polynomials, is given in Supplementary Section S4.1.

The additional conditions strengthen those of
Theorem
3.3
only enough to validate this second-order Edgeworth
expansion. Its first-order consequence is the following limiting-power result.

Corollary 3.6

(Asymptotic power of the WP-based test)
.

Under Assumptions
2.4
–
2.6
and the additional Edgeworth conditions stated in Supplementary Section S2.9.3, if
\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\tau_{n}=\tau\in\mathds{R}^{d_{h}}
,
\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}V_{n}=V\in\mathds{R}^{d_{h}\times d_{h}},
and
\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}W_{n}=W\in\mathds{R}^{d_{h}\times d_{h}},
then we have

\displaystyle\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{P}_{n}^{\star}\left(nR_{n}(\mathbf{h})>\hat{z}_{1-\varrho}\right)=\int_{\left\|v+\tau\right\|_{2}^{2}>z_{1-\varrho}}\bar{\phi}(v)\mathrm{d}v,

where
\bar{\phi}
is the density function of

H\sim\mathcal{N}\big(0,V^{-\frac{1}{2}}WV^{-\frac{1}{2}}\big)
and

z_{1-\varrho}
is the
(1-\varrho)
-quantile of
\left\|H\right\|_{2}^{2}
.

3.4
Power comparison under location shifts

We now specialize the local alternatives in Section
3.3.2
to
location shifts of the data-generating distribution.

Assumption 3.7

(Location-shift alternatives)
.

Let
Y\sim\mathbb{P}_{0}^{\star}\in\mathcal{P}_{0}
and, for a fixed

\tau_{0}\in\mathds{R}^{d_{X}}
, define

X=Y+\frac{\tau_{0}}{\sqrt{n}}\qquad\text{under }\mathbb{P}_{n}^{\star}.

We also impose the mild density regularity stated in
Supplementary Section S2.9.4.1.

The target of this section is to compare the power of the WP-based test with the empirical likelihood test and Hotelling’s
T^{2}
test under the local alternatives in Assumption
3.7
.
The WP-based test is the test in (
WP test
).
For a general
d_{h}
-dimensional moment function
\mathbf{h}
, the empirical likelihood test is defined by

reject
\mathcal{H}_{0}
if
nR^{EL}_{n}(\mathbf{h})>\chi^{2}_{d_{h};1-\varrho}
,

(EL test)

where
\mathcal{R}
is Owen’s support-restricted empirical likelihood ratio, obtained
by reweighting the observed atoms
[
44
, Chapters 2–3]
.
Equivalently,

R^{EL}_{n}(\mathbf{h})=2\inf_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{P}_{0}\\
\mathbb{P}\ll\mathbb{Q}_{n}\end{subarray}}D_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{Q}_{n}\|\mathbb{P})=-\frac{2}{n}\log\mathcal{R},

where
\mathbb{P}\ll\mathbb{Q}_{n}
restricts
\mathbb{P}
to the empirical support.
1
1

1

See
Section
5.1
for further discussion.

The Hotelling-type
T^{2}
test used in the comparison is defined by

reject
\mathcal{H}_{0}
if
\left\langle\mathcal{W}_{n}^{-1},\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{h}(X_{i})\right)^{\otimes 2}\right\rangle>\chi^{2}_{d_{h};1-\varrho}
,

(
T^{2}
test)

where
\mathcal{W}_{n}
is the uncentered empirical second-moment matrix defined in
(
8
). Under the null and the local alternatives considered below,
this statistic differs from the conventional centered Hotelling statistic by

O_{p}(n^{-1})
and is therefore equivalent at the order-
n^{-1/2}
comparison
studied here
[
35
]
.
Fix a confidence level
1-\varrho\in(0,1)
.
The rejection thresholds for the three tests are chosen so that, under the null hypothesis, their Type-I error probabilities are asymptotically equal to
\varrho
.
Then, for a test
s
, we want to compare the local power, defined by the rejection probability under this sequence of alternatives,

\mathbb{P}_{n}^{\star}\left(\text{Test $s$ rejects }\mathcal{H}_{0}\right),\qquad s\in\{\mathrm{WP},\mathrm{EL},T^{2}\}.

This section uses
\mathds{E}_{0}
for the expectation under
\mathbb{P}_{0}^{\star}
.

3.4.1
Vector moment restrictions

We first discuss the comparison when the moment function
\mathbf{h}
is multi-dimensional (
d_{h}>1
).
Let

W_{0}=\mathds{E}_{0}[\mathbf{h}(X)\mathbf{h}(X)^{\top}],\qquad V_{0}=\mathds{E}_{0}[\mathcal{D}\mathbf{h}(X)\Sigma\mathcal{D}\mathbf{h}(X)^{\top}],\qquad\Delta_{0}=\mathds{E}_{0}[\mathcal{D}\mathbf{h}(X)]\tau_{0}.

Here
W_{0}
and
V_{0}
are deterministic population quantities under the null distribution
\mathbb{P}_{0}^{\star}
; they are the null counterparts of the population matrices
W_{n}
and
V_{n}
in Theorem
3.5
, and should not be confused with the random plug-in matrices
\mathcal{W}_{n}
and
\mathcal{V}_{n}
in (
8
).
The vector
\Delta_{0}
denotes the first-order drift of the moment mean induced by the local shift direction
\tau_{0}
.
Under Assumption
3.7
,

\mathds{E}_{n}[\mathbf{h}(X)]=\frac{\Delta_{0}}{\sqrt{n}}+O(n^{-1}),

so the local shift produces a Pitman drift in the moment condition.

We choose the rejection thresholds so that each test has asymptotic confidence level
1-\varrho
under
\mathcal{H}_{0}
.
For the WP-based test, this means using the plug-in threshold
\hat{z}_{1-\varrho}
in (
9
); by Theorem
3.4
,

\mathbb{P}_{0}^{\star}\left(nR_{n}(\mathbf{h})\leq\hat{z}_{1-\varrho}\right)=1-\varrho+\widetilde{O}(n^{-1})=1-\varrho+o(n^{-1/2}).

For EL and Hotelling’s
T^{2}
, the usual chi-square threshold
\chi^{2}_{d_{h};1-\varrho}
is used in (
EL test
) and (

T
2

test
); under the corresponding Wilks and studentized-mean approximations,

\mathbb{P}_{0}^{\star}\left(nR_{n}^{EL}(\mathbf{h})\leq\chi^{2}_{d_{h};1-\varrho}\right)=1-\varrho+o(n^{-1/2}),

and

\mathbb{P}_{0}^{\star}\left(\left\langle\mathcal{W}_{n}^{-1},\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{h}(X_{i})\right)^{\otimes 2}\right\rangle\leq\chi^{2}_{d_{h};1-\varrho}\right)=1-\varrho+o(n^{-1/2}).

We now compare the first-order Type-II errors under the local alternatives.
Let

A_{0}=V_{0}^{-1/2}W_{0}V_{0}^{-1/2},\qquad\tau_{\mathrm{WP}}=V_{0}^{-1/2}\Delta_{0},\qquad\tau_{\mathrm{EL}}=W_{0}^{-1/2}\Delta_{0},

and let
\phi_{d_{h}}
denote the density of
\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d_{h}})
.
The vector
\tau_{\mathrm{WP}}
is the limit of the standardized drift
\tau_{n}=\sqrt{n}\,V_{n}^{-1/2}\mathds{E}_{n}[\mathbf{h}(X)]
in Theorem
3.5
, while
\tau_{\mathrm{EL}}
is the corresponding covariance-standardized drift for EL and Hotelling’s
T^{2}
.
Let
z^{\mathrm{WP}}_{1-\varrho}
be the
(1-\varrho)
-quantile of
Z^{\top}A_{0}Z
for
Z\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d_{h}})
.
Then Theorem
3.5
implies that the Type-II error of the WP-based test satisfies

\mathbb{P}_{n}^{\star}\left(nR_{n}(\mathbf{h})\leq\hat{z}_{1-\varrho}\right)=\int_{\left\|A_{0}^{1/2}z+\tau_{\mathrm{WP}}\right\|_{2}^{2}\leq z^{\mathrm{WP}}_{1-\varrho}}\phi_{d_{h}}(z)\mathrm{d}z+o(1).

For EL and Hotelling’s
T^{2}
, the first-order Type-II errors are both

\int_{\left\|z+\tau_{\mathrm{EL}}\right\|_{2}^{2}\leq\chi^{2}_{d_{h};1-\varrho}}\phi_{d_{h}}(z)\mathrm{d}z+o(1).

Thus, in the multi-dimensional case, WP and EL/Hotelling’s
T^{2}
generally need not have the same first-order local power.
The WP limit involves the non-standard quadratic form
Z^{\top}A_{0}Z
, whereas EL and Hotelling’s
T^{2}
reduce to the usual noncentral chi-square form.
Consequently, a universal ordering is not available in general; for a fixed local shift direction
\tau_{0}
, the test with the smaller first-order Type-II error above has the larger local power.
This first-order comparison issue disappears when
d_{h}=1
: after scalar normalization, the three tests share the same first-order local power, so the comparison can be made through the second-order terms.

3.4.2
Scalar moment restriction

We specialize to a single scalar moment restriction
d_{h}=1
(with
d_{X}

arbitrary) and write the moment function as
h
. The ordering below applies
only to the local location shifts and regularity conditions of
Proposition
3.8
, not to vector moments or other
alternatives.
In this case, the non-standard first-order comparison from the previous subsection collapses to a scalar normalization.
The first-order power term in Theorem
3.5
becomes

\int_{\left\|v+\tau_{n}\right\|_{2}^{2}>z_{n,1-\varrho}}\bar{\phi}(v)\mathrm{d}v=\int_{\left|u+\sqrt{n}\mathds{E}_{n}\left[h(X)\right]/\sqrt{\alpha_{2,n}}\right|^{2}>\chi^{2}_{1;1-\varrho}}\phi(u)\mathrm{d}u,

where
\alpha_{2,n}=\mathds{E}_{n}\left[h(X)^{2}\right]
,
\phi
is the
\mathcal{N}(0,1)
density, and

\chi^{2}_{1;1-\varrho}
is the
(1-\varrho)
-quantile of
\chi_{1}^{2}
. EL and
Hotelling’s
T^{2}
share this first-order power, so only the
n^{-1/2}
terms
distinguish the three tests.

The full second-order WP expansion and its cumulant coefficients are given in
Supplementary Sections S2.9.4 and S4.2; here we retain only the comparison with
EL and Hotelling’s
T^{2}
.
For EL, set
Z_{n,i}=h(X_{i})
. The EL constraint depends on
X_{i}
only
through
Z_{n,i}
, so the statistic is exactly the scalar mean EL statistic for
the transformed observations. A uniform signed-root derivation for the
triangular array
(Z_{n,i})_{i=1}^{n}
is given in Supplementary
Section S2.9.4; its coefficients agree with the fixed-law scalar mean formula
in
[
14
, Theorem 3.1]
.
For Hotelling’s
T^{2}
, the test in (

T
2

test
) is equivalent to

reject
\mathcal{H}_{0}
if
\left\langle\mathcal{V}_{n},\xi_{n}^{\otimes 2}\right\rangle>\hat{z}_{1-\varrho}
,

where
\xi_{n}
and
\mathcal{V}_{n}
are defined in (
6
), and

\hat{z}_{1-\varrho}
is defined in (
9
).
Thus its power calculation is the WP calculation with the cubic tensor

\mathcal{K}_{n}
set to zero.

Let
s\in\{\mathrm{WP},\mathrm{EL},T^{2}\}
index the tests in
(
WP test
), (
EL test
), and (

T
2

test
). The following
proposition gives their pairwise second-order comparison.

Proposition 3.8

(Pairwise second-order power comparison)
.

Suppose Assumption
3.7
and the conditions of
Theorem
3.5
hold with
d_{h}=1
.
Write
\alpha_{j}=\mathds{E}_{0}[h(X)^{j}]
for
j=2,3
and

\tilde{\alpha}_{2}=\mathds{E}_{0}[\mathcal{D}h(X)\Sigma\mathcal{D}h(X)^{\top}]
.
Also set
\tilde{\alpha}_{3}=\mathds{E}_{0}[\mathcal{D}h(X)\Sigma\mathcal{D}^{2}h(X)\Sigma\mathcal{D}h(X)^{\top}]
; the standing
nondegeneracy conditions give
\alpha_{2},\tilde{\alpha}_{2}>0
.
For a confidence level
1-\varrho\in(0,1)
, define

\pi_{n,s}\triangleq\mathbb{P}_{n}^{\star}\left(\text{Test $s$ rejects the null hypothesis $\mathcal{H}_{0}$}\right).

Then, for every
s,t\in\{\mathrm{WP},\mathrm{EL},T^{2}\}
,

\pi_{n,s}-\pi_{n,t}=\frac{B(s)-B(t)}{\sqrt{n}}+o(n^{-1/2}),

where

B(s)=\begin{cases}\frac{\tilde{\alpha}_{3}}{\tilde{\alpha}_{2}^{2}}\times\sqrt{\alpha_{2}}\,I(\varrho,\tau)&\text{if }s=\mathrm{WP},\\[6.45831pt]
\frac{2\alpha_{3}}{3\alpha_{2}^{2}}\times\sqrt{\alpha_{2}}\,I(\varrho,\tau)&\text{if }s=\mathrm{EL},\\
0&\text{if }s=T^{2}.\end{cases}

In the comparison score
B(s)
above,
\phi
denotes the standard-normal
density. Set
\tau=\mathds{E}_{0}[\mathcal{D}h(X)]\tau_{0}/\sqrt{\alpha_{2}}
and

w_{1}=\sqrt{\chi^{2}_{1;1-\varrho}}-\tau
,

w_{2}=\sqrt{\chi^{2}_{1;1-\varrho}}+\tau
, and define

I:(0,1)\times\mathds{R}\to\mathds{R}
by

\displaystyle I(\varrho,\tau)

\displaystyle\triangleq\int_{|v+\tau|^{2}>\chi^{2}_{1;1-\varrho}}\left\{\frac{1+\tau^{2}}{2}v+\tau(v^{2}-1)+\frac{1}{2}(v^{3}-3v)\right\}\phi(v)\mathrm{d}v

\displaystyle=\frac{\chi^{2}_{1;1-\varrho}}{2}\{\phi(w_{1})-\phi(w_{2})\}.

Moreover, all three tests have the common limiting local power

\lim_{n\to\infty}\pi_{n,s}=\int_{|v+\tau|^{2}>\chi^{2}_{1;1-\varrho}}\phi(v)\mathrm{d}v.

The leading local power is common to all three tests and therefore does not
determine a preference among WP, EL, and Hotelling’s
T^{2}
; their pairwise
power differences are governed by the second-order scores
B(s)-B(t)
.
Because
\mathbb{P}_{n}^{\star}
differs from
\mathbb{P}_{0}^{\star}
by an
O(n^{-1/2})
location shift,
the relevant empirical averages are
O_{p}(n^{-1/2})
-close to their

\mathbb{P}_{0}^{\star}
counterparts: both their sampling error and their shift bias have
that order. Away from the nondegenerate denominators in
Proposition
3.8
, plug-in therefore changes
B(s)
by

O_{p}(n^{-1/2})
and the estimated
n^{-1/2}
power gap by
O_{p}(n^{-1})
.
This statement concerns estimation of the comparison scores, not replacement
of a population critical value by
\hat{z}_{1-\varrho}
. Thus, for a specified

\tau_{0}
, the scores have a direct plug-in implementation; an unspecified

\tau_{0}
requires structural information or an external estimator. A
data-driven ranking is stable away from
\tau_{h}=0
and pairwise score ties.

Let
\tau_{h}=\mathds{E}_{0}[\mathcal{D}h(X)]\tau_{0}
and define the sign-adjusted scores

C_{\mathrm{WP}}=\mathrm{sign}(\tau_{h})\frac{\tilde{\alpha}_{3}}{\tilde{\alpha}_{2}^{2}},\qquad C_{\mathrm{EL}}=\mathrm{sign}(\tau_{h})\frac{2\alpha_{3}}{3\alpha_{2}^{2}}.

If
c=\sqrt{\chi^{2}_{1;1-\varrho}}
, then

\phi(c-\tau)-\phi(c+\tau)=2\phi(c)e^{-\tau^{2}/2}\sinh(c\tau),

so
I(\varrho,\tau)
has the same sign as
\tau_{h}
. The second-order
ranking is determined by Table
1
. When

\tau_{h}=0
, both scores vanish and all three tests agree to this order.

Table 1:
Second-order local-power scores (larger is better; ties indicate equal power).

Test

Second-order score

Preferred when

WP

C_{\mathrm{WP}}

C_{\mathrm{WP}}\geq\max\{C_{\mathrm{EL}},0\}

EL

C_{\mathrm{EL}}

C_{\mathrm{EL}}\geq\max\{C_{\mathrm{WP}},0\}

Hotelling’s
T^{2}

0

0\geq\max\{C_{\mathrm{WP}},C_{\mathrm{EL}}\}

(a) Second-order local-power comparison.

(b) Moment shapes and unshifted null density.

Figure 1:
Pairwise local-power comparison. Panel (a) plots

\sqrt{n}(\widehat{\pi}_{n,s}-\widehat{\pi}_{n,T^{2}})
for
s\in\{\mathrm{WP},\mathrm{EL}\}
using common random samples,
n=6000
,
\tau_{0}=3
, and

300{,}000
replications. Shading gives paired pointwise 95% intervals.
Dashed curves are the limiting scores
B(s)-B(T^{2})
from
Proposition
3.8
, solid curves are finite-
n
moment
refinements that account for the exact shifted moments, and the backgrounds
mark the asymptotically predicted winner. Panel (b) overlays three representative moment
functions with the unshifted null density (gray fill and dashed curve);
each title gives the ordering of the corresponding comparison scores.
Full details are in Supplementary Section S2.2.

The WP score measures the gradient-weighted curvature
\tilde{\alpha}_{3}

relative to the squared gradient energy
\tilde{\alpha}_{2}^{2}
, whereas the EL
score measures value skewness
\alpha_{3}
relative to the squared moment
variance
\alpha_{2}^{2}
. The selection rule compares their sign-adjusted ratios

\tilde{\alpha}_{3}/\tilde{\alpha}_{2}^{2}
and
2\alpha_{3}/(3\alpha_{2}^{2})
, using zero as
the
T^{2}
benchmark.
Figure
1
makes the mechanism concrete under a
smooth symmetric three-component Gaussian-mixture null with

h_{\theta}(x)=x^{2}+(\theta/3)x-1
and
\tau_{0}=3
. Here

\tau_{h}=\theta
and
h_{\theta}^{\prime\prime}\equiv 2
.
For
\theta<0
, the induced moment drift is negative and both sign-adjusted
ratios are negative, so
T^{2}
is preferred; at
\theta=0
, all scores vanish. For

0<\theta<\theta_{\star}
, the fixed curvature and the gradient energy

\tilde{\alpha}_{2}=4+\theta^{2}/9
, which remains close to its minimum, make the
WP gradient-curvature ratio substantially larger; at
\theta=1
the WP ratio
is more than twice the EL ratio. As
\theta
increases, the transformed
moment becomes more value-skewed; after the crossing at

\theta_{\star}=2.03423
, the EL value-skewness ratio is larger (its standardized
skewness is
1.084
at
\theta=3
), so EL is preferred. All calculations and
simulation details are in Supplementary Section S2.2.

3.5
Higher-order asymptotic expansion and Bartlett-type correction

3.5.1
Higher-order asymptotic expansion

As a natural extension of the second-order expansion in Theorem
3.1
,
this section expands the WP statistic through order
n^{-1}
, with an

\widetilde{O}(n^{-3/2})
remainder, using the proof strategy detailed in
Supplementary Sections S2.9.6–S2.9.9 under suitable regularity conditions.

The expansion to this order and the resulting Edgeworth expansion refine (
7
) by identifying the
n^{-1}
term. This result is closely related to the Bartlett-type correction
[
19
]
, which removes that term and therefore improves on the approximation in Theorem
3.2
.

Higher-order expansions require additional smoothness and moment conditions beyond those in Section
2.2
. To focus on demonstrating the proof strategy and avoid cumbersome assumptions, we impose fourth-order smoothness together with global at-most-quadratic growth and exponential-moment control of the relevant envelopes.

Assumption 3.9

(Global growth, smoothness, and exponential moments)
.

We assume that
\mathbf{h}\in C^{4}(\mathds{R}^{d_{X}})
and that the global
Jacobian-Lipschitz condition in Assumption
2.5
holds with envelope

\kappa_{1}
. Define the fourth-order envelope, for
x\in\mathds{R}^{d_{X}}
, by

\mathcal{E}_{4}(x)\triangleq\left\|\mathbf{h}(x)\right\|_{2}+\kappa_{1}(x)+\sum_{j=1}^{4}\sup_{\left\|u-x\right\|_{2}\leq 1}\left\|\mathcal{D}^{j}\mathbf{h}(u)\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}.

We assume that there exists
\bar{\eta}>0
such that

\mathds{E}\left[\exp\left\{\bar{\eta}\mathcal{E}_{4}(X)\right\}\right]<\infty.

The global part of this assumption is essential for a squared transport cost:
it prevents a vanishing amount of mass from being sent to infinity to repair a
superquadratic moment at arbitrarily small cost. The local derivative terms
provide the higher-order Taylor envelopes, while inclusion of
\kappa_{1}(X)

supplies the concentration bounds used in the localization argument.

We next present the higher-order asymptotic expansion of
nR_{n}(\mathbf{h})
.

Theorem 3.10

(Higher-order expansion of
nR_{n}(\mathbf{h})
)
.

Under Assumptions
2.4
,
2.7
,
2.8
, and
3.9
, there is a deterministic sequence
\hat{\delta}_{n}=\widetilde{O}\left(n^{-\frac{3}{2}}\right)
and an integer
N
such that, when
n\geq N
,

\displaystyle nR_{n}(\mathbf{h})=\left\langle\mathcal{V}_{n},\xi_{n}^{\otimes 2}\right\rangle+\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\left\langle\mathcal{K}_{n},\xi_{n}^{\otimes 3}\right\rangle+\frac{1}{n}\left\langle\mathcal{L}_{n},\xi_{n}^{\otimes 4}\right\rangle+\hat{\varepsilon}_{n}.

(12)

The remainder satisfies

\mathbb{P}^{\star}\left(\left|\hat{\varepsilon}_{n}\right|\leq\hat{\delta}_{n}\right)=1-O(n^{-3/2})
.
The explicit fourth-order tensor
\mathcal{L}_{n}
is defined in
Supplementary Section S2.9.6.

With the expansion (
12
) in hand, we can apply the same analysis as in the discussion before Theorem
3.2
. Thus, the higher-order Edgeworth expansion and the related Bartlett-type correction are applicable, as we will demonstrate for the one-dimensional case
d_{h}=1
in the next section.

3.5.2
Bartlett-type correction

In the WP-based test, we use an estimated quantile of the limiting distribution of

nR_{n}(\mathbf{h})
as the rejection threshold for the null hypothesis
\mathcal{H}_{0}
.
As noted earlier, the resulting confidence level is correct only asymptotically,
which can produce size distortions
[
16
, Chapter 2]
.
Expanding the actual confidence level in powers of
n^{-1}
reveals its rate of
convergence to the target level. Once the second-order term is identified, a
Bartlett correction
[
2
]
can adjust the statistic and
improve this rate. In particular, under the null, the distribution of the
corrected statistic is generally approximated more accurately by the original
limiting distribution, thereby reducing small-sample size distortion.

To obtain an explicit second-order expansion term, in contrast to Theorem
3.4
, we need to expand
nR_{n}(\mathbf{h})
(available in Theorem
3.10
) and the estimated quantile
\hat{z}_{1-\varrho}
to higher orders. For simplicity, we present the result when
d_{h}=1
, and thus, we write
\mathbf{h}
as
h
. Note that in this case,
\hat{z}_{1-\varrho}=(\mathcal{W}_{n}/\mathcal{V}_{n})\chi^{2}_{1;1-\varrho}
, for
\mathcal{W}_{n},\mathcal{V}_{n}\in\mathds{R}
.

Theorem 3.11

(Coverage accuracy of the WP test II)
.

Under Assumptions
2.4
,
2.7
,
2.8
, and
3.9
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}^{\star}\left(nR_{n}(h)\leq\hat{z}_{1-\varrho}\right)=1-\varrho+\frac{1}{n}g_{1}(\chi^{2}_{1;1-\varrho})q(\chi^{2}_{1;1-\varrho})+\widetilde{O}\left(n^{-\frac{3}{2}}\right),

where
g_{1}(\cdot)
and
\chi^{2}_{1;1-\varrho}
are the density and

(1-\varrho)
-quantile of the chi-square distribution with one degree of
freedom, and
q(t)=\sum_{k=1}^{3}C_{k}t^{k}
. The coefficients
C_{k}
and their
moment and cumulant definitions are collected in
Supplementary Sections S2.9.10 and S4.3.

Theorem
3.11
implies that a simple correction can be applied to the quantile
\hat{z}_{1-\varrho}
to remove the
n^{-1}
term. This leads to the first correction presented in the following proposition.

Proposition 3.12

(Bartlett-type correction I)
.

Under Assumptions
2.4
,
2.7
,
2.8
, and
3.9
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}^{\star}\left(nR_{n}(h)\leq\left(1-C\right)\hat{z}_{1-\varrho}\right)=1-\varrho+\widetilde{O}\left(n^{-\frac{3}{2}}\right),

where
C=\sum_{k=1}^{3}C_{k}\left(\chi^{2}_{1;1-\varrho}\right)^{k-1}/n
.

The correction in Proposition
3.12
is generally level-dependent, since
C
depends on the target quantile
\chi^{2}_{1;1-\varrho}
. The classical Bartlett correction
[
2
]
, by contrast, rescales the test statistic by a single factor that is the same for all confidence levels. This classical form is recovered from Proposition
3.12
when
B_{2}=B_{3}=0
, where
B_{2}
and
B_{3}
are defined in Supplementary Section S2.9.10. In this case
C_{2}=C_{3}=0
, so
C=C_{1}/n
is independent of
\varrho
, and the correction can be written directly as

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}^{\star}\left(\left(1-C\right)^{-1}nR_{n}(h)\leq\hat{z}_{1-\varrho}\right)=1-\varrho+\widetilde{O}\left(n^{-\frac{3}{2}}\right).

(13)

This Bartlett correction to the test statistic uses the same constant
C
for all confidence levels
1-\varrho\in(0,1)
, and is valid only when
B_{2}=B_{3}=0
(see the discussion in
[
23
, Section 2.3]
).

Nevertheless, even when the original Bartlett correction is invalid, another general Bartlett-type correction proposed by
[
17
]
can be applied to the test statistic. Specifically, we adopt their construction to get

Proposition 3.13

(Bartlett-type correction II)
.

For
\mathcal{V}_{n},\mathcal{W}_{n}\in\mathds{R}
defined in (
8
), let

S=\frac{\mathcal{V}_{n}}{\mathcal{W}_{n}}nR_{n}(h).

Under Assumptions
2.4
,
2.7
,
2.8
, and
3.9
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}^{\star}\left(\left(1+\frac{1}{n}\sum_{k=1}^{3}C_{k}S^{k-1}\right)nR_{n}(h)\leq\hat{z}_{1-\varrho}\right)=1-\varrho+\widetilde{O}\left(n^{-\frac{3}{2}}\right).

(14)

Note that the correction in Proposition
3.13
involves the random variable
S
, in contrast to the original Bartlett correction in (
13
); nevertheless, the same corrected statistic is used for all confidence levels
1-\varrho\in(0,1)
. In practice, the moments entering
C_{k}
,
k=1,2,3
, may be replaced by their empirical counterparts under
\mathbb{Q}_{n}
. Under additional concentration conditions ensuring that the resulting estimators satisfy
\widehat{C}_{k}-C_{k}=\widetilde{O}_{p}(n^{-1/2})
, together with suitable local anti-concentration bounds, the induced perturbation of the corrected statistic is of order
\widetilde{O}_{p}(n^{-3/2})
. Accordingly, Propositions
3.12
–
3.13
are stated for the population coefficients
C_{k}
, while the simulation below uses their empirical counterparts.

To conclude this section, we present a simulation to illustrate the improvement of coverage using the corrections in Propositions
3.12
and
3.13
.

Example 3.14

(Gaussian quadratic moment function)
.

Consider the one-dimensional quadratic moment function

h(x)=x^{2}-1,\mathbb{P}^{\star}=\mathcal{N}(0,1),

with squared Euclidean ground cost and nominal coverage
1-\varrho=0.95
.
The normal design makes the centered moment
h(X)=X^{2}-1
substantially skewed, so the first-order chi-square approximation exhibits a visible finite-sample coverage error. In each replication, every moment entering
C_{1},C_{2},C_{3}
is estimated from the same sample used to compute the test statistic. Table
2
reports Monte Carlo coverage probabilities based on
2\times 10^{6}
replications; the numbers in parentheses are Monte Carlo standard errors.

h(x)=x^{2}-1
,

, nominal coverage
0.95
, and

replications. Entries are percentages; parentheses report Monte Carlo standard errors.

Both plug-in corrections reduce undercoverage at every reported sample size, with Correction II closer to nominal coverage throughout. The remaining undercoverage is most pronounced at
n=50
and decreases as
n
grows.

4
Computation for WP tests

We develop a certified computational scheme for the WP test. At nominal significance level

\varrho\in(0,1)
, the test rejects when
nR_{n}(\mathbf{h})>\hat{z}_{1-\varrho}
.

A direct approach would compute the exact value of
R_{n}(\mathbf{h})
from its unscaled dual
representation derived in Supplementary Section S2.7.2. It can be written compactly as

\displaystyle R_{n}(\mathbf{h})=-\inf_{\zeta\in\mathds{R}^{d_{h}}}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sup_{x\in\mathds{R}^{d_{X}}}\left[\zeta^{\top}\mathbf{h}(x)-\left\|x-X_{i}\right\|_{\Sigma}^{2}\right].

(15)

This is a convex–nonconcave minimax problem: the outer objective is convex in
\zeta
, whereas
the inner maximization need not be concave in
x
for a nonlinear moment function
\mathbf{h}
. For
the WP test, however, computing the exact projection distance is unnecessary. Given the
rejection threshold
\delta=\hat{z}_{1-\varrho}/n
, it suffices to decide whether

R_{n}(\mathbf{h})>\delta
. We first recast this comparison as a distributionally robust optimization
(DRO) problem over the Wasserstein ball centered at
\mathbb{Q}_{n}

(Proposition
4.1
). We then show that, when
\delta
falls below an
explicit observable threshold, the dual of the DRO problem localizes to a compact box on which
the inner minimizations are strongly convex and the maximization over the direction variable is
strongly concave (Proposition
4.2
). These two facts certify the nested
first-order scheme detailed as Algorithm 1 in Supplementary Section S2.10.4, whose total cost is
\widetilde{O}(n)
with probability
1-O(n^{-1})
under the regularity and accuracy conditions stated below and whose statistical
behavior provably matches that of the oracle test up to
\widetilde{O}(n^{-1})

(Theorem
4.3
). When higher-order accuracy is required, as with a
Bartlett correction, the same scheme can be adapted by tightening the optimization tolerance
while retaining its
\widetilde{O}(n)
total cost with high probability; details are given in Supplementary Section S2.10.7. In short,
the proposed simplification reduces the computational burden without compromising the
statistical validity of the test.

We begin by reformulating the threshold comparison. For
\delta>0
, let

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{\delta}(\mathbb{Q}_{n})\triangleq\left\{\mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathds{R}^{d_{X}}):\mathds{W}_{c}(\mathbb{Q}_{n},\mathbb{P})\leq\delta\right\},\qquad\mathbb{B}_{2}\triangleq\left\{\xi\in\mathds{R}^{d_{h}}:\left\|\xi\right\|_{2}\leq 1\right\},

(16)

and define, for
\alpha\geq 0
and
\xi\in\mathds{R}^{d_{h}}
,

\displaystyle F_{\delta}(\alpha,\xi)\triangleq-\alpha\delta+\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\inf_{x\in\mathds{R}^{d_{X}}}\left[\xi^{\top}\mathbf{h}(x)+\alpha\left\|x-X_{i}\right\|_{\Sigma}^{2}\right],\qquad\Phi_{n}(\delta)\triangleq\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\geq 0\\
\xi\in\mathbb{B}_{2}\end{subarray}}F_{\delta}(\alpha,\xi).

(17)

The function
F_{\delta}
is possibly extended-valued and jointly concave in
(\alpha,\xi)
,
because it is an average of pointwise infima of affine functions. It is also positively
homogeneous along rays:

\displaystyle F_{\delta}(t\alpha,t\xi)=t\,F_{\delta}(\alpha,\xi)\qquad\text{for all }t>0.

(18)

Since
F_{\delta}(0,\mathbf{0})=0
, we always have
\Phi_{n}(\delta)\geq 0
. The following proposition
shows that the sign of
\Phi_{n}(\delta)
exactly determines the WP testing decision.

Proposition 4.1

(DRO reformulation of the WP threshold comparison)
.

Suppose Assumptions
2.4
and
2.5
hold, and let
\delta>0
. Then:

(i)

The following strong-duality identity holds:

\displaystyle\Phi_{n}(\delta)=\inf_{\mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{B}_{\delta}(\mathbb{Q}_{n})}\,\max_{\xi\in\mathbb{B}_{2}}\,\mathds{E}_{\mathbb{P}}\!\left[\xi^{\top}\mathbf{h}(X)\right]=\inf_{\mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{B}_{\delta}(\mathbb{Q}_{n})}\left\|\mathds{E}_{\mathbb{P}}[\mathbf{h}(X)]\right\|_{2}\geq 0,

(19)

(ii)

The threshold comparison is equivalent to the sign of the dual value:

\displaystyle R_{n}(\mathbf{h})>\delta\ \Longleftrightarrow\ \Phi_{n}(\delta)>0.

(20)

The proof is given in Supplementary Section S2.10.1. To turn this reformulation
into an algorithm, we next introduce the curvature and nondegeneracy quantities used to localize
the dual problem. Assumption
2.5
provides the Hessian envelope
\kappa_{1}
; we impose the
uniform strengthening

\displaystyle\overline{\kappa}_{1}\triangleq\sup_{x\in\mathds{R}^{d_{X}}}\kappa_{1}(x)<\infty,\qquad K_{\mathrm{curv}}\triangleq\left\|\Sigma\right\|_{2}\,\overline{\kappa}_{1},

(21)

which implies the uniform directional Hessian bound

\displaystyle\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in\mathds{R}^{d_{X}}\\
\left\|u\right\|_{2}=1\end{subarray}}\left\|\Sigma^{1/2}\mathcal{D}^{2}\!\left(u^{\top}\mathbf{h}\right)(x)\Sigma^{1/2}\right\|_{2}\leq K_{\mathrm{curv}}.

(22)

For nondegeneracy, recall the empirical Jacobian Gram matrix
\mathcal{V}_{n}
from
Theorem
3.1
and define its extreme eigenvalues by

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}_{n}\triangleq\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathcal{D}\mathbf{h}(X_{i})\Sigma\mathcal{D}\mathbf{h}(X_{i})^{\top},\qquad\ell_{n}\triangleq\lambda_{\min}(\mathcal{V}_{n}),\qquad U_{n}\triangleq\lambda_{\max}(\mathcal{V}_{n}),

(23)

and write
A(x)\triangleq\mathcal{D}\mathbf{h}(x)\Sigma^{\frac{1}{2}}\in\mathds{R}^{d_{h}\times d_{X}}
, so that

\mathcal{V}_{n}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}A(X_{i})A(X_{i})^{\top}
. The deterministic results below are stated on
the observable event
\ell_{n}>0
, whose probability approaches one under
Assumptions
2.8
and
2.9
.

Although
\Phi_{n}(\delta)
is a concave maximization, its multiplier range
\alpha\geq 0
is
unbounded. The next proposition localizes the maximizers to a compact box. On the event

\ell_{n}>0
, define

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \varkappa_{n}&\triangleq\frac{U_{n}}{\ell_{n}}\geq 1,&\underline{\alpha}_{\delta}&\triangleq\frac{1}{2}\left(\sqrt{\frac{\ell_{n}}{\delta}}-K_{\mathrm{curv}}\right),\\
\overline{\alpha}_{\delta}&\triangleq\frac{1}{2}\left(\sqrt{\frac{U_{n}}{\delta}}+K_{\mathrm{curv}}\right),&\lambda(\alpha)&\triangleq 2\alpha-K_{\mathrm{curv}},\\
\delta_{0}&\triangleq\frac{\ell_{n}}{K_{\mathrm{curv}}^{2}\left(3+2\sqrt{\varkappa_{n}}\right)^{2}},&\mathcal{K}_{\delta}&\triangleq\left[\underline{\alpha}_{\delta},\overline{\alpha}_{\delta}\right]\times\mathbb{B}_{2},\end{aligned}

(24)

with
\delta_{0}\triangleq+\infty
when
K_{\mathrm{curv}}=0
.
The box endpoints scale as
\delta^{-1/2}
: in the testing regime

\delta=\hat{z}_{1-\varrho}/n
, the relevant transport multipliers
\alpha
are
bracketed between constant multiples of

\sqrt{n\ell_{n}/\hat{z}_{1-\varrho}}
and

\sqrt{nU_{n}/\hat{z}_{1-\varrho}}
. In particular, when the empirical condition
number
\varkappa_{n}=U_{n}/\ell_{n}
remains bounded, they are of exact order

\sqrt{n\ell_{n}/\hat{z}_{1-\varrho}}
, a fact that drives both the conditioning of
the dual problem and the accuracy accounting below.

Proposition 4.2

(Localization and curvature of the dual problem)
.

Suppose Assumptions
2.4
and
2.5
hold,
(
21
) is satisfied, and
\ell_{n}>0
. Let
0<\delta\leq\delta_{0}
, and
define the localized dual value

\displaystyle\Phi^{\mathrm{loc}}_{n}(\delta)\triangleq\max_{(\alpha,\xi)\in\mathcal{K}_{\delta}}F_{\delta}(\alpha,\xi).

(25)

Then:

(i)

(Localization.)
The supremum defining
\Phi_{n}(\delta)
is attained, and

\displaystyle\Phi_{n}(\delta)=\max\left\{\Phi^{\mathrm{loc}}_{n}(\delta),\,0\right\}.

(26)

If
\Phi_{n}(\delta)>0
, then every maximizer
(\alpha^{\star},\xi^{\star})
of
\Phi_{n}(\delta)

satisfies
\left\|\xi^{\star}\right\|_{2}=1
and
(\alpha^{\star},\xi^{\star})\in\mathcal{K}_{\delta}
, so that

\Phi^{\mathrm{loc}}_{n}(\delta)=\Phi_{n}(\delta)
in this case. Consequently,

\displaystyle R_{n}(\mathbf{h})>\delta\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\Phi^{\mathrm{loc}}_{n}(\delta)>0.

(27)

(ii)

(Strong convexity in
x
.)
For every
\alpha\geq\underline{\alpha}_{\delta}
,

\xi\in\mathbb{B}_{2}
, and
i\in[n]
, the inner objective

x\mapsto\xi^{\top}\mathbf{h}(x)+\alpha\left\|x-X_{i}\right\|_{\Sigma}^{2}
is
\lambda(\alpha)
-strongly
convex and
(2\alpha+K_{\mathrm{curv}})
-smooth with respect to
\left\|\cdot\right\|_{\Sigma}
, with

\displaystyle\lambda(\alpha)\geq\sqrt{\frac{\ell_{n}}{\delta}}-2K_{\mathrm{curv}}\geq\frac{3}{5}\sqrt{\frac{\ell_{n}}{\delta}}>0;

(28)

(iii)

(Strong concavity in
\xi
.)
For every
\alpha\geq\underline{\alpha}_{\delta}
,
the function
\xi\mapsto F_{\delta}(\alpha,\xi)
is
\mu(\alpha)
-strongly concave on

\mathbb{B}_{2}
, where

\displaystyle\mu(\alpha)\triangleq\frac{\left(\sqrt{\ell_{n}}-\dfrac{K_{\mathrm{curv}}\sqrt{U_{n}}}{\lambda(\alpha)}\right)^{2}}{2\alpha+K_{\mathrm{curv}}}\geq\frac{\ell_{n}}{\left(1+\sqrt{\varkappa_{n}}\right)^{2}(2\alpha+K_{\mathrm{curv}})}>0.

(29)

The proof is given in Supplementary Section S2.10.2;
Assumption
2.4
enters only through part (i), where it rules out maximizers at

\alpha=0
. The localization identity (
26
) is also important
computationally. Because
\Phi_{n}(\delta)
is nonnegative, the entire event

\{R_{n}(\mathbf{h})\leq\delta\}
is mapped to
\Phi_{n}(\delta)=0
; thus, under the null, the
distribution of
\Phi_{n}(\delta)
has an atom at zero corresponding to the non-rejection event.
This atom makes a direct numerical comparison of
\Phi_{n}(\delta)
with zero delicate.

The localized value
\Phi_{n}^{\mathrm{loc}}(\delta)
retains the sign information lost through
this truncation. If
R_{n}(\mathbf{h})<\delta
, choose a null distribution
\mathbb{P}_{0}
satisfying

\mathds{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{0}}[\mathbf{h}(X)]=\mathbf{0}
and a coupling with
\mathbb{Q}_{n}
whose transportation cost
r
is
less than
\delta
. For every

(\alpha,\xi)\in\mathcal{K}_{\delta}
, weak duality gives

F_{\delta}(\alpha,\xi)\leq-\alpha\delta+\xi^{\top}\mathds{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{0}}[\mathbf{h}(X)]+\alpha r=-\alpha(\delta-r)\leq-\underline{\alpha}_{\delta}(\delta-r)<0,

and hence
\Phi_{n}^{\mathrm{loc}}(\delta)<0
. Conversely,
(
27
) gives
\Phi_{n}^{\mathrm{loc}}(\delta)>0
whenever

R_{n}(\mathbf{h})>\delta
. Consequently,

\{\Phi_{n}^{\mathrm{loc}}(\delta)=0\}\subseteq\{R_{n}(\mathbf{h})=\delta\}.

Under the standard no-ties condition
\mathbb{P}^{\star}(R_{n}(\mathbf{h})=\delta)=0
, the localized value
therefore has no atom at zero, making it the more useful quantity for computation.

4.1
A certified nested algorithm

The homogeneity in (
18
) prevents joint strong concavity on
full-dimensional domains, so the
algorithm uses the partial curvature in Proposition
4.2
. It searches
over the scalar multiplier
\alpha
, applies projected gradient ascent in
\xi
, and solves the

n
strongly convex inner problems in parallel. On
\mathcal{K}_{\delta}
, the resulting
strong-concavity-to-smoothness condition number in
\xi
can be taken as

30\varkappa_{n}^{3/2}
and therefore has no explicit dependence on
n
when the empirical condition
number
\varkappa_{n}=U_{n}/\ell_{n}
remains bounded. Explicit moduli, data-scale constants, stopping
rules, and the complete complexity ledger are given in Supplementary
Sections S2.10.3–S2.10.6.

Theorem 4.3

(Computable WP test)
.

Suppose Assumptions
2.4
and
2.5
hold and
(
21
) is satisfied. Set

\delta=\hat{z}_{1-\varrho}/n
and let
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{alg},n}>0
. On the observable event

\displaystyle\mathfrak{E}_{n}:\qquad\ell_{n}>0,\qquad\delta\leq\delta_{0}/2,\qquad\varepsilon_{\mathrm{alg},n}\leq\sqrt{\ell_{n}\delta}/8,

(30)

run Algorithm 1 in Supplementary Section S2.10.4 and let
\hat{\Phi}_{n}
be its
returned value. Define the computable test
\hat{\mathsf{T}}_{n}
to reject
when
\mathfrak{E}_{n}
holds and
\hat{\Phi}_{n}>\varepsilon_{\mathrm{alg},n}
, and to fail to reject otherwise. Define

w_{n}=5\varepsilon_{\mathrm{alg},n}\sqrt{2n\hat{z}_{1-\varrho}/\ell_{n}}
on
\mathfrak{E}_{n}
and set
w_{n}=0
on

\mathfrak{E}_{n}^{c}
. Then:

(i)
On
\mathfrak{E}_{n}
,

|\hat{\Phi}_{n}-\Phi_{n}^{\mathrm{loc}}(\delta)|\leq\varepsilon_{\mathrm{alg},n}
.

(ii)
Deterministically,

\displaystyle\{\text{computable test rejects}\}

\displaystyle\subseteq\{nR_{n}(\mathbf{h})>\hat{z}_{1-\varrho}\},

\displaystyle\{nR_{n}(\mathbf{h})>\hat{z}_{1-\varrho}\}\cap\mathfrak{E}_{n}

\displaystyle\subseteq\{\text{computable test rejects}\}

\displaystyle\quad\cup\bigl(\{0<nR_{n}(\mathbf{h})-\hat{z}_{1-\varrho}\leq w_{n}\}\cap\mathfrak{E}_{n}\bigr).

(31)

Equivalently, the second inclusion holds globally after adjoining
\mathfrak{E}_{n}^{c}
to its
right-hand side. Thus failure of the observable certification event is explicitly retained
in the decision comparison.

(iii)
Suppose additionally that
d_{h}
is fixed,
Assumptions
2.4
–
2.10
hold, and

\varepsilon_{\mathrm{alg},n}^{-1}=O(n^{c})
for some fixed
c>0
. There exist events

\mathfrak{C}_{n}
with
\mathbb{P}^{\star}(\mathfrak{C}_{n}^{c})=O(n^{-1})
such that, on

\mathfrak{E}_{n}\cap\mathfrak{C}_{n}
, the algorithm uses

O(n\log^{3}n)=\widetilde{O}(n)
first-order oracle calls, where one call jointly evaluates

(\mathbf{h},\mathcal{D}\mathbf{h})
at a single point. For the accuracy specified in part
(iv)
,

\mathbb{P}^{\star}(\mathfrak{E}_{n}\cap\mathfrak{C}_{n})=1-O(n^{-1})
.

(iv)
For the statistical conclusions, take

\varepsilon_{\mathrm{alg},n}=1/(n^{3/2}\log^{2}n)
. Under
Assumptions
2.4
–
2.10
and
(
21
), the computable test inherits the oracle test’s size
expansion up to
\widetilde{O}(n^{-1})
. For each fixed true parameter value at
which these assumptions hold, its inverted confidence region likewise inherits
the oracle pointwise coverage expansion up to
\widetilde{O}(n^{-1})
. Under the assumptions of
Theorem
3.5
, together with
(
21
), it likewise inherits the oracle local-power expansion up to

\widetilde{O}(n^{-1})
. Finally, when
d_{h}=1
, under the assumptions of
Proposition
3.12
, together with
(
21
), taking

\varepsilon_{\mathrm{alg},n}=1/(n^{2}\log^{2}n)
preserves the corrected test’s

\widetilde{O}(n^{-3/2})
coverage accuracy. Under the same assumptions, the
corrected algorithm also uses
O(n\log^{3}n)
first-order oracle calls with
probability
1-O(n^{-3/2})
.

Thus computation is simplified without altering the statistical guarantees at the order
studied in the paper. The factor
\varkappa_{n}^{3/2}
also has a statistical interpretation:
when
\ell_{n}
is nearly zero, both the optimization problem and the limiting WP law are nearly
degenerate. The proof, complete complexity ledger, stopping rules, and pseudocode are given
in Supplementary Sections S2.10.4 and S2.10.6.

Supplementary Section S2.3 provides a complementary finite-dimensional stress test in
which the data dimension, number of moment restrictions, and sample size all vary. Across
the tested designs, the literal certified algorithm maintains null rejection rates close to
the nominal level, local power close to its fixed-dimensional limit, and certification
frequencies of at least
0.996
; its decisions also closely track the leading quadratic
WP statistic. These results support both the finite-sample calibration predicted by the
theory and the reliability of the certified implementation beyond the scalar experiment below.

4.2
Numerical experiment: fairness testing

We now implement the certified nested procedure of Section
4.1

(Algorithm 1 in the Supplementary Material) in a nonlinear fairness test. Let

A\in\{-1,1\}
be a protected attribute and
X\in\mathds{R}
a score. For
k>0
, the
null requires parity of the groups’ mean transformed scores:

\mathds{E}[\tanh(kX)\mid A=1]=\mathds{E}[\tanh(kX)\mid A=-1].

The bounded monotone transformation interpolates between smooth mean-score
parity for small
k
and parity of positive decisions near the threshold zero
for large
k
.

We consider a prospective balanced audit of a fixed scoring system. Before
observing scores, the auditor draws
n
cases independently from each of two
fixed group-specific sampling frames and independently randomizes the order
within each group. Combining observations with the same index gives

Z_{j}=(X_{j,+},X_{j,-}),\qquad j=1,\ldots,n.

This pairing is only bookkeeping and does not assert covariate matching. The
vectors
Z_{j}
are i.i.d. from
P_{+}\otimes P_{-}
, and the group roles are fixed
coordinate labels. Thus the sample consists of
n
i.i.d. paired vectors. Define

\displaystyle\widetilde{h}_{k}(Z_{j})

\displaystyle=\frac{\tanh(kX_{j,+})-\tanh(kX_{j,-})}{2k},

\displaystyle\widetilde{c}_{\mathrm{aniso}}(z,z^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}(z_{+}-z^{\prime}_{+})^{2}+5(z_{-}-z^{\prime}_{-})^{2},

\displaystyle\widetilde{c}_{\mathrm{id}}(z,z^{\prime})

\displaystyle=(z_{+}-z^{\prime}_{+})^{2}+(z_{-}-z^{\prime}_{-})^{2}.

The factor
1/k
does not change the null or the WP projection, but prevents
the derivative scale from collapsing as
k
decreases. To evaluate the
geometry-dependent second-order prediction of
Proposition
3.8
, we compare

\Sigma_{\mathrm{aniso}}=\operatorname{diag}(2,0.2)
with

\Sigma_{\mathrm{id}}=I_{2}
. Both procedures use the same moment restriction,
samples, and asymptotic chi-square reference quantile and differ only in
transport geometry; the fixed group-coordinate labels cannot be exchanged.
The identity geometry is
therefore a matched control, while the anisotropic geometry is chosen because
the theory predicts a larger favorable second-order correction without a
change in first-order power. The two matrices are thus controlled design
choices for evaluating the theoretical prediction. Both costs are of the
Mahalanobis form in (
2
). Under either geometry,

R_{n}(\widetilde{h}_{k})
is the least average quadratic score movement needed to
attain transformed-score parity, so the theory applies with i.i.d. sample size

n
.

The redesigned experiment uses
k=0.5
and

n\in\{75,100,125,150,250,500\}
paired observations. Under the null, the coordinates
are independent with

\displaystyle Y_{+}

\displaystyle\sim 0.338\mathcal{N}(-7.60,0.1^{2})+0.662\mathcal{N}(-1.319,0.1^{2}),

(32)

\displaystyle Y_{-}

\displaystyle\sim\mathcal{N}(-1.817,0.1^{2}).

The displayed parameters are rounded. The negative-group location was
obtained by Gaussian quadrature so that

\mathds{E}[\tanh(0.5Y_{+})]=\mathds{E}[\tanh(0.5Y_{-})]\approx-0.720
. The far-left mixture
component lies in the nearly flat lower tail of the transformation, where

\tanh(0.5y)
is close to
-1
, whereas the other component lies near the point
of maximal positive curvature. Numerical quadrature gives
the second-order comparison scores

C_{\mathrm{WP,aniso}}=2.216,\qquad C_{\mathrm{WP,id}}=0.228,\qquad C_{\mathrm{EL}}=-2.018,\qquad C_{T^{2}}=0,

so Proposition
3.8
predicts a pronounced advantage for
anisotropic WP and only a small advantage for identity WP over
T^{2}
.
Under the local alternative,

X_{+}=Y_{+}+\frac{\tau}{2\sqrt{n}},\qquad X_{-}=Y_{-}-\frac{\tau}{2\sqrt{n}},\qquad\tau\approx 1.759.

(33)

Writing

\alpha_{2}=\mathds{E}[\widetilde{h}_{0.5}(Y_{+},Y_{-})^{2}]
, this value makes the
standardized first-order drift

\frac{\tau\{\mathds{E}[\operatorname{sech}^{2}(0.5Y_{+})]+\mathds{E}[\operatorname{sech}^{2}(0.5Y_{-})]\}}{4\sqrt{\alpha_{2}}}=2.

Consequently the experiment targets common first-order limiting power

\mathbb{P}(|Z+2|>1.96)=0.516
and uses curvature, rather than different first-order
signal strengths, to distinguish the methods.

To complement this local comparison with a fixed-alternative consistency
check, we also use

X_{+}=Y_{+}+\frac{\Delta}{2},\qquad X_{-}=Y_{-}-\frac{\Delta}{2},\qquad\Delta=\frac{\tau}{\sqrt{150}}\approx 0.144.

(34)

Thus the fixed and local alternatives coincide at
n=150
; the fixed
alternative is weaker when
n<150
and stronger when
n>150
. At each
n
,
the two alternatives use the same underlying paired draws
(Y_{+},Y_{-})
.

For the certified test, let
\widetilde{h}_{j}=\widetilde{h}_{0.5}(Z_{j})
and define

\widehat{W}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\widetilde{h}_{j}^{2},\qquad\widehat{V}_{\Sigma}=\frac{1}{4n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\{\sigma_{+}\operatorname{sech}^{4}(0.5X_{j,+})+\sigma_{-}\operatorname{sech}^{4}(0.5X_{j,-})\},

where
(\sigma_{+},\sigma_{-})=(2,0.2)
for anisotropic WP and
(1,1)

for identity WP. The resulting plug-in radius is

\delta=\widehat{W}\chi^{2}_{1;0.95}/(n\widehat{V}_{\Sigma})
. The required
derivatives are available in closed form, and
the global curvature bound

K_{\mathrm{curv}}=2k\max(\sigma_{+},\sigma_{-})/(3\sqrt{3})
makes the certification
event
\mathfrak{E}_{n}
fully observable. It held in
17{,}953
of
18{,}000

anisotropic-WP replications and all
18{,}000
identity-WP replications;
uncertified runs were conservatively counted as non-rejections.

Table
3
reports the common-reference power
comparison. Each design uses
1000
common samples under the null and each
alternative. All four methods use the common asymptotic reference quantile

\chi^{2}_{1;0.95}=3.841
; WP converts it to the appropriate sample-specific
radius and uses the literal certified algorithm with

\varepsilon_{\mathrm{alg},n}=1/(n^{3/2}\log^{2}n)
, while EL-infeasible samples count as rejections.
This common reference directly evaluates the finite-sample ordering predicted
by Proposition
3.8
, with the observed null rejection
rates reporting any finite-sample calibration differences.

Panel A treats the local alternative and Panel B the fixed alternative. Null
rejection rates are similar across methods, while anisotropic WP has the
highest estimated power throughout. For
n=75
through
150
in Panel A,
its power exceeds EL by 3.9–5.0 percentage points,
T^{2}
by 2.9–4.0 points,
and identity WP by 2.6–3.5 points; identity WP exceeds
T^{2}
by only
0.1–0.6 points across all six sizes. The local gaps diminish with
n
, as predicted by the
order-
n^{-1/2}
correction, whereas fixed-alternative powers approach one.
The two panels coincide at
n=150
, providing an implementation check. These
design-specific results do not imply general dominance; Figure
1

shows geometries that instead favor EL or
T^{2}
.

uses

and

uses

; both use literal Algorithm 1. Panel A uses local separation
\tau/\sqrt{n}
,

, and Panel B uses fixed separation
\tau/\sqrt{150}=0.144
, so the panels coincide at

common samples; parentheses give Monte Carlo standard errors.
