5
Discussion

The results reveal two roles for transport geometry: locally, it enters the
higher-order distribution and power expansions through derivatives of the
moment function; computationally, it yields the localized nested formulation
of Section
4
. We next relate WP to empirical
likelihood, discuss nonlocal power, and outline extensions beyond i.i.d. sampling.

5.1
WP and EL: geometry versus reweighting

Empirical likelihood (EL) also projects onto the null: it minimizes

D_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{Q}_{n}\|\mathbb{P})
over laws supported on the observed atoms,
equivalently maximizing empirical likelihood. With the normalization in
(
EL test
),

R_{n}^{\mathrm{EL}}(\mathbf{h})=2\inf_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{P}_{0}\\
\mathbb{P}\ll\mathbb{Q}_{n}\end{subarray}}D_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{Q}_{n}\|\mathbb{P})=-\frac{2}{n}\log\mathcal{R};

see
[
44
, Chapters 2–3]
. The local dual expansions have
the same algebraic form but different tensors: EL uses the observed values of

\mathbf{h}
and generates
W
, whereas WP uses derivatives of
\mathbf{h}
and the
ground-cost matrix
\Sigma
and generates
V
.

At the next order, the curvature scores
\tilde{\alpha}_{3}/\tilde{\alpha}_{2}^{2}
for
WP and
2\alpha_{3}/(3\alpha_{2}^{2})
for EL determine their relative power; see
Proposition
3.8
. The complete dual expansion, its
quadratic through quartic coefficients, and the consistency check against

[
14
, Theorem 3.1]
appear in Supplementary Section S2.4.1.

The interpolation proposed by
[
6
]
suggests a broader
family that tunes the balance between geometric and value-based information;
its higher-order analysis is a natural direction for future work.

5.2
From local to nonlocal power

The local comparison and fairness experiment reflect the same mechanism: EL
and Hotelling’s
T^{2}
use only
\{\mathbf{h}(X_{i})\}
, whereas WP also uses the
sample-space geometry. This distinction persists nonlocally. Supplementary
Sections S2.4.1–S2.4.2 construct smooth alternatives along which the population
EL and
T^{2}
discrepancies converge to zero while the WP discrepancy stays bounded away
from zero. All three tests are pointwise consistent, but only WP retains
uniform separation along the sequence.

5.3
Extensions beyond i.i.d. sampling and computational scope

The deterministic expansion and optimization do not intrinsically require
independence, but inferential extensions need a joint Edgeworth expansion and
high-probability control of the regularity event; relevant tools include

[
46
,
52
,
39
,
1
]
.
The computation remains valid conditional on the observable localization
event, whereas dependent-data inference also needs a valid critical-value
approximation. The certified algorithm decides only whether
R_{n}(\mathbf{h})

exceeds the testing threshold; it does not reconstruct a minimizing projected
law or provide statistical inference for that optimizer. We therefore make no condition-free claim for arbitrary
non-i.i.d. sequences.

6
Conclusions

We developed higher-order theory and certified computation for WP tests of
moment restrictions. The expansions yield

\widetilde{O}(n^{-1})
size accuracy, an order-
n^{-1/2}
local-power
correction, and scalar Bartlett-type corrections with

\widetilde{O}(n^{-3/2})
coverage accuracy. WP’s second-order power reflects
derivative-based curvature and geometry, whereas EL’s reflects moment
skewness. The fairness experiment illustrates this contrast without implying
dominance. Finally, we propose a certified localized dual algorithm that
implements the WP test with oracle accuracy and high-probability

\widetilde{O}(n)
complexity.

S2
Technical development

Relation to statistical limit theory for optimal transport

Classical empirical-transport limit theory typically fixes one or two target
laws and studies fluctuations of a full transport distance such as

\mathds{W}_{c}(\mathbb{Q}_{n},\mathbb{P})
or
\mathds{W}_{c}(\mathbb{Q}_{n},\mathbb{P}_{m})
. The resulting behavior is
sensitive to the support, dimension, cost, and whether the two population laws
coincide. On finite spaces, Sommerfeld and Munk
[
53
]
obtained generally
non-Gaussian limits from directional Hadamard differentiability; related work
covers countable metric spaces and Gaussian models

[
54
,
47
]
. For quadratic transport in
general Euclidean dimension, del Barrio and Loubes
[
21
]
established Gaussian
central limit theorems, centered by the expected empirical cost, using
uniqueness and stability of Kantorovich potentials. This centering is
material: the bias between the expected empirical cost and the population
transport cost can decay at a dimension-dependent empirical-transport rate
and need not be negligible on the
n^{-1/2}
scale

[
26
,
58
]
. Hundrieser, Klatt, Munk, and
Staudt
[
36
]
subsequently developed a unifying
empirical-process limit theory for optimal transport. Under Donsker
conditions on the relevant class of Kantorovich potentials, their theory
centers directly at the population transport cost and expresses the limits as
suprema of Gaussian processes. These results describe first-order
fluctuations of the unrestricted transport functional around a fixed pair of
laws.

WP concerns a different functional,

R_{n}(\mathbf{h})=\inf_{\mathbb{P}:\,\mathds{E}_{\mathbb{P}}[\mathbf{h}(X)]=\mathbf{0}}\mathds{W}_{c}(\mathbb{Q}_{n},\mathbb{P}).

Under the null, the population law itself belongs to the target set, so the
first variation vanishes and the leading term comes from a local quadratic
projection onto the finite-dimensional moment constraint. Consequently,

R_{n}(\mathbf{h})
is of order
n^{-1}
and
nR_{n}(\mathbf{h})
has a quadratic-form limit
whose effective dimension is
d_{h}
, rather than the ambient sample-space
dimension
d_{X}

[
7
,
50
]
. The expansions in
the main paper refine this projection regime through Edgeworth and
Bartlett-type corrections; they are therefore complementary to, rather than a
replacement for, limit theory for the unrestricted empirical Wasserstein
distance.

Other dimension-robust transport discrepancies change the geometry itself.
Entropic regularization yields Sinkhorn divergences with a parametric

n^{-1/2}
estimation rate for fixed regularization, with constants that
deteriorate as the regularization is removed
[
28
]
.
Gaussian-smoothed Wasserstein distances convolve both laws before transport
and admit parametric rates and functional limit theory in any fixed dimension

[
43
,
29
]
. Sliced Wasserstein distances
instead aggregate one-dimensional projected transport problems, for which a
uniform process limit gives distributional results for several slicing rules

[
59
]
. WP retains the original ground cost and obtains
its parametric behavior from projection onto finitely many restrictions, while
Sinkhorn, smoothing, and slicing trade some of the original transport geometry
for statistical or computational regularity.

This contrast can also be seen from the dual representations. In the
unifying theory of Hundrieser, Klatt, Munk, and
Staudt
[
36
]
, unrestricted transport is a supremum over
a class of Kantorovich potentials, and a population-centered limit theorem
requires the corresponding empirical process to be Donsker. Whether this
holds depends on the dimension and the complexity of the potential class; in
high dimension its metric entropy can be too large, which is an
empirical-process manifestation of the curse of dimensionality. The modified
transport discrepancies reduce this dual complexity in different ways. For
fixed regularization, Sinkhorn confines the relevant dual optimizers to a
bounded Sobolev–RKHS ball
[
28
]
. Gaussian smoothing moves
the convolution to the dual test functions, thereby regularizing the indexed
class sufficiently for parametric empirical-process behavior under suitable
conditions
[
43
,
29
]
. Slicing replaces the
ambient-dimensional transport problem by a uniform process of one-dimensional
transport problems indexed by projection directions
[
59
]
.
By contrast, the local WP dual is indexed by the finite-dimensional multiplier

\zeta\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{h}}
associated with the prescribed moment map
\mathbf{h}
.
Thus its null fluctuations are controlled by the number and regularity of the
moment restrictions, rather than by the full class of ambient-dimensional
Kantorovich potentials. In this paper, this finite-dimensional dual
representation underlies both the stochastic expansions and the computational
algorithm developed below.

S2.1
Supplementary details for Section 2

S2.1.1
Exact rescaled dual representation

The following exact formula underlies the local quadratic approximation in
Section 2.

Proposition S2.1

(Rescaling of the WP function)
.

Suppose that
\mathbf{h}\in C^{1}(\mathds{R}^{d_{X}})
is bounded and that the moment constraint is feasible:

\mathbf{0}\in\inte\left(\conv\{\mathbf{h}(x):x\in\mathds{R}^{d_{X}}\}\right).

For
\mathbb{Q}_{n}=n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\delta_{X_{i}}
and

c(\bar{x},x)=\left\|\bar{x}-x\right\|_{\Sigma}^{2}
,

\displaystyle nR_{n}(\mathbf{h})=\sup_{\zeta\in\mathds{R}^{d_{h}}}\left\{-\zeta^{\top}H_{n}-M_{n}(\zeta)\right\},\qquad H_{n}\triangleq\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{h}(X_{i}),

(S.1)

where

\displaystyle M_{n}(\zeta)\triangleq\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sup_{\Delta\in\mathds{R}^{d_{X}}}\left\{\zeta^{\top}\int_{0}^{1}\mathcal{D}\mathbf{h}(X_{i}+n^{-1/2}\Delta u)\Delta\,\mathrm{d}u-\left\|\Delta\right\|_{\Sigma}^{2}\right\}.

S2.1.2
Comments on the regularity conditions

Letting
\Delta\to 0
in Assumption 2.5 gives

\Big\|\sum_{\beta\in[d_{h}]}\zeta^{(\beta)}\mathcal{D}^{2}h^{\beta}(x)\Big\|_{2}\leq\kappa_{1}(x)\left\|\zeta\right\|_{2},

so
\kappa_{1}
controls the Hessian of
\mathbf{h}
in every scalar direction.

Assumption 2.5 is compatible with moment functions whose growth at
infinity is at most quadratic, matching the squared Mahalanobis cost
in (2). This compatibility prevents the WP distance from
degenerating. For example, suppose
d_{X}=d_{h}=1
,
\mathbb{Q}_{n}=\delta_{1}
, and

h(x)=x^{p}
for odd
p>2
. For

\mathbb{P}_{\varepsilon}=(1-\varepsilon)\delta_{1}+\varepsilon\delta_{-((1-\varepsilon)/\varepsilon)^{1/p}},

we have
\mathds{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{\varepsilon}}[h(X)]=0
, whereas

\mathds{W}_{c}(\mathbb{Q}_{n},\mathbb{P}_{\varepsilon})\leq\varepsilon\left(1+\left((1-\varepsilon)/\varepsilon\right)^{1/p}\right)^{2}=O\left(\varepsilon^{1-2/p}\right)\to 0.

Thus a superquadratic moment can be repaired by transporting an arbitrarily
small amount of mass over an arbitrarily large distance at vanishing quadratic
cost. One may instead take

c(\bar{x},x)=\ell(\left\|\bar{x}-x\right\|_{2})
, with
\ell(r)
behaving like
r^{p}
at
infinity and like
r^{2}
near zero. Because the expansion is driven by local
perturbations of the empirical atoms, the same local analysis applies where
this modified cost remains quadratic.

The smoothness requirement in Assumption 2.6 is structural rather than merely technical.
For example, the fairness constraint studied by Si et al.
[
51
]
has an
estimating function that is discontinuous at a decision boundary. In one dimension, when
the sampling density is positive near the boundary,
O_{p}(n^{1/2})
observations lie within
distance
O_{p}(n^{-1/2})
of the discontinuity. Their boundary-layer mechanism moves only
these observations by
O_{p}(n^{-1/2})
, rather than perturbing the entire sample. With the
squared ground cost used in the main paper, the resulting heuristic scale is

O_{p}(n^{1/2}\cdot n^{-1}\cdot n^{-1})=O_{p}(n^{-3/2})
, instead of the

O_{p}(n^{-1})
scale in the smooth case. Si et al.
[
51
]
use a first-order
transport cost and correspondingly obtain an
O_{p}(n^{-1})
projection. Thus dropping
smoothness changes both the scaling and the geometry of the projection and requires a
different analysis.

The eighth-moment envelope in Assumption 2.9 yields the
uniform bounds used in the stochastic expansion. For example, Markov’s
inequality and a union bound give

\mathbb{P}^{\star}\left(\max_{i\in[n]}\kappa_{1}(X_{i})>n^{1/4}\right)\leq n\frac{\mathds{E}[\kappa_{1}(X)^{8}]}{n^{2}}=O(n^{-1}),

and hence
\max_{i\in[n]}\kappa_{1}(X_{i})/\sqrt{n}=o_{p}(1)
.

Assumption 2.10 is tailored to the Edgeworth expansion of

\sqrt{n}(\mathcal{K}_{n}-\mathds{E}[\mathcal{K}_{n}])
. Each summand contributing to
\mathcal{K}_{n}
is
bounded, up to constants, by

\left\|\mathcal{D}\mathbf{h}(X_{i})\right\|_{2}^{2}\kappa_{1}(X_{i})
, so its fourth moment is controlled by
the displayed product moment in that assumption. This is the moment order
needed for an order-
n^{-1}
Edgeworth expansion; see

[
5
, Theorem 20.1]
. The Cramér regularity in
Assumption 2.7 supplies the accompanying nonlattice condition; useful
sufficient conditions appear in Section
S2.6
.

S2.2
Simulation design for Figure 1 of the main paper

The numerical comparison uses the smooth symmetric three-component
Gaussian-mixture null

\mathbb{P}_{0}^{\star}=\tfrac{1}{4}\mathcal{N}(-\mu,\sigma^{2})+\tfrac{1}{2}\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^{2})+\tfrac{1}{4}\mathcal{N}(\mu,\sigma^{2}),\qquad\sigma^{2}=0.005,\qquad\mu=\sqrt{2(1-\sigma^{2})}=\sqrt{1.99}.

It is symmetric with mean zero and variance one. The moment function is

h_{\theta}(x)=x^{2}+\frac{\theta}{3}x-1,

so
\mathds{E}_{0}[h_{\theta}(X)]=0
and

h_{\theta}^{\prime}(x)=2x+\frac{\theta}{3},\qquad h_{\theta}^{\prime\prime}(x)=2,\qquad h_{\theta}^{(3)}(x)=h_{\theta}^{(4)}(x)=0.

Thus the smoothness and moment conditions used for the local-power expansion
hold for every fixed
\theta
. Direct calculation gives

\displaystyle\mathds{E}_{0}[X^{4}]

\displaystyle=2+2\sigma^{2}-\sigma^{4},

\displaystyle\mathds{E}_{0}[X^{6}]

\displaystyle=4+18\sigma^{2}-3\sigma^{4}-4\sigma^{6},

\displaystyle\kappa

\displaystyle\triangleq\mathds{E}_{0}[(X^{2}-1)^{2}]=1+2\sigma^{2}-\sigma^{4},

\displaystyle\lambda

\displaystyle\triangleq\mathds{E}_{0}[(X^{2}-1)^{3}]=12\sigma^{2}-4\sigma^{6},

and hence

\displaystyle\alpha_{2}

\displaystyle=\mathds{E}_{0}[h_{\theta}(X)^{2}]=\kappa+\frac{\theta^{2}}{9},

\displaystyle\alpha_{3}

\displaystyle=\mathds{E}_{0}[h_{\theta}(X)^{3}]=\lambda+\frac{\kappa\theta^{2}}{3},

\displaystyle\tilde{\alpha}_{2}

\displaystyle=\mathds{E}_{0}[(h_{\theta}^{\prime}(X))^{2}]=4+\frac{\theta^{2}}{9},

\displaystyle\tilde{\alpha}_{3}

\displaystyle=\mathds{E}_{0}[(h_{\theta}^{\prime}(X))^{2}h_{\theta}^{\prime\prime}(X)]=2\left(4+\frac{\theta^{2}}{9}\right).

For the local shift below,

\tau_{h}=\mathds{E}_{0}[h_{\theta}^{\prime}(X)]\tau_{0}=\theta
. The sign-adjusted scores in
Table 1 of the main paper therefore reduce to

C_{\mathrm{WP}}=\operatorname{sign}(\theta)\frac{2}{4+\theta^{2}/9},\qquad C_{\mathrm{EL}}=\operatorname{sign}(\theta)\frac{2(\lambda+\kappa\theta^{2}/3)}{3(\kappa+\theta^{2}/9)^{2}}.

When
\theta<0
, both are negative and Hotelling’s
T^{2}
is best. For

\theta>0
, the sign of
C_{\mathrm{EL}}-C_{\mathrm{WP}}
is the sign of

d(t)=3(\kappa-1)t^{2}+(\lambda+6\kappa)t+(4\lambda-3\kappa^{2}),\qquad t=\frac{\theta^{2}}{9}.

The polynomial has a unique positive root

t_{\star}\approx 0.45979
, giving
\theta_{\star}\approx 2.03423
.
Thus WP is best for
0<\theta<\theta_{\star}
: throughout this interval

h_{\theta}^{\prime\prime}=2
is fixed and

\tilde{\alpha}_{2}=4+\theta^{2}/9
stays close to its minimum
4
, so

C_{\mathrm{WP}}
remains large. At
\theta=1
, for example,

C_{\mathrm{WP}}=0.48649>0.21040=C_{\mathrm{EL}},

making the WP advantage pronounced rather than marginal. Beyond the crossing,
the EL score is larger. At
\theta=3
the conventional skewness of the
transformed moment is

\frac{\alpha_{3}}{\alpha_{2}^{3/2}}=1.08433,\qquad C_{\mathrm{EL}}=0.50989>C_{\mathrm{WP}}=0.40000,

which exhibits the value-skewness mechanism favoring EL. At
\theta=0
,

\tau_{h}=0
and all three tests tie to order
n^{-1/2}
.

We take
n=6000
and
\tau_{0}=3
, so the observations under the alternative
are shifted by
\tau_{0}/\sqrt{n}\approx 0.03873
. We evaluate
\theta
on an equally
spaced grid from
-4
to
4
in increments of
1/12
, including the tie point
zero. Every WP sample is calibrated with the feasible threshold

\hat{z}_{1-\varrho}=\frac{n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}h_{\theta}(X_{i})^{2}}{n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}h_{\theta}^{\prime}(X_{i})^{2}}\chi^{2}_{1;1-\varrho}

analyzed in Proposition 3.8. Panel (a) reports

\sqrt{n}(\widehat{\pi}_{n,s}-\widehat{\pi}_{n,T^{2}})
for

s\in\{\mathrm{WP},\mathrm{EL}\}
and overlays the corresponding theoretical
scores
B(s)-B(T^{2})
from that proposition. The rejection probabilities are
estimated from
300{,}000
Monte Carlo replications using common random samples
across all values of
\theta
and random seed 20260806. The WP projection is
evaluated by its exact quadratic-moment formula

R_{n}(h_{\theta})=\left\{\sqrt{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(X_{i}+\theta/6)^{2}}-\sqrt{1+\theta^{2}/36}\right\}^{2},

and the EL statistic by its exact scalar dual equation. Raw estimates are
shown as markers. Shaded regions are pointwise
paired 95% Monte Carlo intervals, computed from the sample variance of the
within-replication difference of rejection indicators; the largest standard
error on the displayed
\sqrt{n}
scale is
0.0126
.

The dashed comparison scores are limits and omit the order-
n^{-1}
term in
the power difference. That term is unusually visible here because the
quadratic moment produces the exact drift

m_{1,n}\triangleq\mathds{E}_{n}[h_{\theta}(X)]=\frac{\theta}{\sqrt{n}}+\frac{9}{n},\qquad\sqrt{n}\,m_{1,n}=\theta+\frac{9}{\sqrt{n}}.

Thus at
n=6000
the finite-sample drift on the standardized-mean scale
contains the addition
9/\sqrt{n}\approx 0.116
. This is not Monte Carlo
or EL root-solving error: the largest residual in the EL dual equation is

5.0\times 10^{-12}
. To display the resulting pre-asymptotic effect, let

m_{j,n}=\mathds{E}_{n}[h_{\theta}(X)^{j}],\qquad\widetilde{m}_{2,n}=\mathds{E}_{n}[(h_{\theta}^{\prime}(X))^{2}],\qquad\tau_{n}^{\mathrm{ref}}=\frac{\sqrt{n}\,m_{1,n}}{\sqrt{m_{2,n}}},

and put
q=\chi^{2}_{1;1-\varrho}
and
c=\sqrt{q}
. The solid curves use the
finite-
n
moment refinements

\displaystyle B_{\mathrm{WP},n}^{\mathrm{ref}}

\displaystyle=\frac{q\sqrt{m_{2,n}}}{\widetilde{m}_{2,n}}\left\{\phi(c-\tau_{n}^{\mathrm{ref}})-\phi(c+\tau_{n}^{\mathrm{ref}})\right\},

\displaystyle B_{\mathrm{EL},n}^{\mathrm{ref}}

\displaystyle=\frac{qm_{3,n}}{3m_{2,n}^{3/2}}\left\{\phi(c-\tau_{n}^{\mathrm{ref}})-\phi(c+\tau_{n}^{\mathrm{ref}})\right\}.

They evaluate the same score functionals at the exact shifted moments and
converge to the dashed Proposition 3.8 scores. Their difference from those
limits is
O(n^{-1/2})
on the displayed scale, hence
O(n^{-1})
on the
unscaled power scale covered by the proposition’s remainder. For example, at

\theta=1
the limiting EL score is
0.09942
, the refinement is
0.15799
,
and the simulated scaled gap is
0.15518
. At
\theta=3
the corresponding
values are
0.54708
,
0.60513
, and
0.61271
. The refinement therefore
explains the apparent upward displacement of EL without changing the
asymptotic ranking rule. The background shading continues to mark the three
limiting regimes derived above. Panel (b) uses
\theta=-3
,
1
, and
3
and
overlays each
h_{\theta}
with the density of the unshifted law

\mathbb{P}_{0}^{\star}
. The gray fill and dashed curve show this density, while each
panel title reports the ordering of
C_{\mathrm{WP}}
,
C_{\mathrm{EL}}
, and
the
T^{2}
benchmark zero.

S2.3
Certified finite-dimensional stress test

We examine the finite-sample behavior of the WP test as the data dimension

d_{X}
, the number of moment restrictions
d_{h}
, and the sample size
n
vary.
Every decision reported below uses the literal fixed-loop implementation of
Algorithm 1, including its observable certification event and its
fail-to-reject convention off that event. No direct projection, KKT solver,
adaptive stopping rule, or warm start is used.

The design is

\displaystyle X

\displaystyle\sim\mathcal{N}(\mu_{n},I_{d_{X}}),

\displaystyle\mathbf{h}_{k}(X)

\displaystyle=\frac{\tanh(kBX)}{k},

\displaystyle BB^{\top}

\displaystyle=I_{d_{h}},

\displaystyle k

\displaystyle=0.5,

(S.2)

where
\tanh
acts componentwise. Under the null,
\mu_{n}=0
. Under the
local alternative,

\displaystyle\mu_{n}=\frac{2.5}{\sqrt{nd_{h}}}B^{\top}\mathbf{1}_{d_{h}},

(S.3)

so
\sqrt{n}\left\|\mu_{n}\right\|_{2}=2.5
for every
d_{h}
. We use

d_{X}\in\{5,10,20\}
,
d_{h}\in\{1,2,5\}
, and

n\in\{150,250,500\}
. Hence all nine dimension pairs are nondegenerate.
The scaling
k=0.5
retains a smooth nonlinear moment while reducing the
global Jacobian Lipschitz constant to

K_{\mathrm{curv}}=4k/(3\sqrt{3})=0.3849
, which makes the observable certification condition
informative at these sample sizes.

For every sample, the plug-in critical value is computed in the general form
of equations (8)–(9). In particular,

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}_{n}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{h}_{k}(X_{i})\mathbf{h}_{k}(X_{i})^{\top},

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}_{n}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathcal{D}\mathbf{h}_{k}(X_{i})\mathcal{D}\mathbf{h}_{k}(X_{i})^{\top},

and
\hat{z}_{0.95}
is the
0.95
quantile of

\sum_{j=1}^{d_{h}}\hat{\lambda}_{j}Z_{j}^{2}
, where the
Z_{j}
are independent standard
normals and the
\hat{\lambda}_{j}
are the eigenvalues of

\mathcal{V}_{n}^{-1/2}\mathcal{W}_{n}\mathcal{V}_{n}^{-1/2}
. We evaluate this quantile
deterministically by a positive-mixture expansion with coefficient-tail
tolerance
10^{-13}
.

We set
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{alg},n}=1/\{n^{3/2}\log^{2}(n)\}
. Algorithm 1 first checks

\displaystyle\mathfrak{E}_{n}:\qquad\ell_{n}>0,\qquad\hat{z}_{0.95}/n\leq\delta_{0}/2,\qquad\varepsilon_{\mathrm{alg},n}\leq\sqrt{\ell_{n}\hat{z}_{0.95}/n}/8,

and fails to reject when this event does not hold. On
\mathfrak{E}_{n}
, it
uses exactly the prescribed
k^{\star}
trisection rounds,
T

projected-gradient steps, and
k_{\mathrm{in}}
preconditioned-gradient
steps, restarting every inner problem at
X_{i}
and every profile query at

\xi=0
. It rejects precisely when the returned

\widehat{\Phi}_{n}>\varepsilon_{\mathrm{alg},n}
.

The implementation stores the score
z=Bx
during the inner iterations. This
is an exact coordinate representation of Algorithm 1: starting from
x=X_{i}
,
every preconditioned-gradient displacement lies in the row space of
B
, and
multiplication by
B
maps the ambient iterate bijectively to the score
iterate. Consequently, decisions and loop counts are invariant to
d_{X}
for
fixed
(d_{h},n)
; only the arithmetic cost of an ambient first-order oracle
call depends on
d_{X}
. Dense deterministic orthonormal-row matrices for all
nine
(d_{X},d_{h})
pairs are retained with the simulation archive.

For orientation, let
Z\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)
and define

\displaystyle a

\displaystyle=\mathds{E}[\operatorname{sech}^{2}(kZ)],

\displaystyle w

\displaystyle=\mathds{E}\left[\{\tanh(kZ)/k\}^{2}\right],

\displaystyle v

\displaystyle=\mathds{E}[\operatorname{sech}^{4}(kZ)].

Here
a=0.8265
,
w=0.6941
, and
v=0.7174
. Thus the null matrices are

W=wI_{d_{h}}
and
V=vI_{d_{h}}
, and the WP limiting local-power calculation
reduces to a noncentral chi-square distribution with common noncentrality

\displaystyle\lambda=2.5^{2}a^{2}/w=6.1510.

The limiting local power is therefore

\mathbb{P}\{\chi^{2}_{d_{h}}(\lambda)>\chi^{2}_{d_{h};0.95}\}
, which equals
0.6985
,

0.5954
, and
0.4433
for
d_{h}=1,2,5
, respectively.

Each null and local-alternative cell uses
1{,}000
Monte Carlo samples. The
Gaussian score arrays are nested across
n
within each
(d_{h},\text{stage})

block, so changes across sample sizes can also be assessed with paired
comparisons. Table
S.3
reports each

d_{X}
-invariant result once.

for

, with paired standard errors

,

,
and

. Thus the size and local-power results are stable across the

As an additional link to the asymptotic expansion, we recomputed the leading

H_{n}^{\top}\mathcal{V}_{n}^{-1}H_{n}
using the same plug-in critical value. Its decisions
disagree with literal Algorithm 1 in at most
0.006
of any cell, and in at
most
0.002
of any null cell. Hence the reported behavior is not driven by
an optimization shortcut or by certification failures: the literal certified
algorithm closely tracks the leading statistic in these samples.

S2.4
Supplementary details for Section 5

S2.4.1
Dual coefficients in the WP–EL comparison

With the Wilks normalization used by the EL test, empirical likelihood can be written as

R_{n}^{\mathrm{EL}}(\mathbf{h})=2\inf_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{P}_{0}\\
\mathbb{P}\ll\mathbb{Q}_{n}\end{subarray}}D_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{Q}_{n}\|\mathbb{P})=-2n^{-1}\log\mathcal{R}(\theta)
when
\mathbf{h}(x)=\mathbf{f}(\theta,x)
; see

[
44
, Chapters 2–3]
. Here
\mathbb{P}\ll\mathbb{Q}_{n}
is Owen’s
support restriction to laws that reweight the observed atoms. The WP and EL dual representations
(see
[
44
, Section 12.3]
for EL) have local expansions of the form

\displaystyle nR_{n}^{(s)}(\mathbf{h})

\displaystyle=\{F_{n}^{(s)}\}^{*}(-H_{n})+\widetilde{O}_{p}(n^{-1}),\qquad s\in\{\mathrm{WP},\mathrm{EL}\},

where
R_{n}^{(\mathrm{WP})}=R_{n}
,
H_{n}=n^{-1/2}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{h}(X_{i})
, and

\displaystyle F_{n}^{(s)}(\zeta)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bigg\{A^{s}_{\omega^{1}\omega^{2}}(X_{i})\zeta^{(\omega^{1})}\zeta^{(\omega^{2})}+\frac{A^{s}_{\omega^{1}\omega^{2}\omega^{3}}(X_{i})}{\sqrt{n}}\zeta^{(\omega^{1})}\zeta^{(\omega^{2})}\zeta^{(\omega^{3})}

\displaystyle\hskip 119.50157pt+\frac{A^{s}_{\omega^{1}\omega^{2}\omega^{3}\omega^{4}}(X_{i})}{n}\prod_{j=1}^{4}\zeta^{(\omega^{j})}\bigg\}.

The quadratic and cubic coefficients are compared in Table
S.5
.

Table S.5:
Quadratic and cubic coefficients in the WP and EL dual expansions. All
derivatives and moment values are evaluated at
X_{i}
.

Coefficient

WP

EL

A^{s}_{\omega^{1}\omega^{2}}

\frac{1}{4}h^{\omega^{1}}_{\beta}h^{\omega^{2}}_{\gamma}\Sigma_{\beta\gamma}

\frac{1}{4}h^{\omega^{1}}h^{\omega^{2}}

A^{s}_{\omega^{1}\omega^{2}\omega^{3}}

\frac{1}{8}h^{\omega^{1}}_{\beta}h^{\omega^{2}}_{\lambda\eta}h^{\omega^{3}}_{\gamma}\Sigma_{\beta\lambda}\Sigma_{\eta\gamma}

\frac{1}{12}h^{\omega^{1}}h^{\omega^{2}}h^{\omega^{3}}

The quartic coefficients, which enter the
n^{-1}
coverage term and the Bartlett-type
corrections, are

\displaystyle A^{\mathrm{WP}}_{\omega^{1}\omega^{2}\omega^{3}\omega^{4}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{16}h^{\omega^{1}}_{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}h^{\omega^{2}}_{\beta^{\prime}\gamma^{\prime}}h^{\omega^{3}}_{\beta}h^{\omega^{4}}_{\omega^{\prime}}\Sigma_{\omega^{\prime}\gamma^{\prime}}\Sigma_{\beta\alpha}\Sigma_{\beta^{\prime}\alpha^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{1}{48}h^{\omega^{1}}_{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}\alpha^{\prime\prime}}h^{\omega^{2}}_{\beta}h^{\omega^{3}}_{\beta^{\prime}}h^{\omega^{4}}_{\beta^{\prime\prime}}\Sigma_{\beta\alpha}\Sigma_{\beta^{\prime}\alpha^{\prime}}\Sigma_{\beta^{\prime\prime}\alpha^{\prime\prime}},

\displaystyle A^{\mathrm{EL}}_{\omega^{1}\omega^{2}\omega^{3}\omega^{4}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{32}h^{\omega^{1}}h^{\omega^{2}}h^{\omega^{3}}h^{\omega^{4}}.

All quantities are evaluated at the corresponding observation
X_{i}
. Replacing

\tilde{\alpha}_{2}
by
\alpha_{2}
and
\tilde{\alpha}_{3}
by
2\alpha_{3}/3
in the WP
expansion gives its EL analogue. Substitution into the scalar power expansion recovers

[
14
, Theorem 3.1]
through order
n^{-1/2}
.

Example S2.2

(Uniform separation by transport geometry)
.

For squared Euclidean ground cost, there exist a smooth even function

h:\mathds{R}\to[-1,2]
, points
x_{0},x_{1},\ldots
, and
distributions
\mathbb{P}^{(k)}=\frac{1}{2}\delta_{x_{0}}+\frac{1}{2}\delta_{x_{k}}
,
k\geq 1
, such that, for

\mathcal{P}_{0}=\{\mathbb{P}:\mathds{E}_{\mathbb{P}}[h(X)]=0\}
,

\displaystyle\left|\mathds{E}_{\mathbb{P}^{(k)}}[h(X)]\right|^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4^{k+1}}\longrightarrow 0,

(S.4a)

\displaystyle\inf_{\mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{P}_{0}}D_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}^{(k)}\|\mathbb{P})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\log\left(1+\frac{1}{2^{k+1}}\right)+\frac{1}{2}\log\left(1-\frac{1}{2(2^{k}+1)}\right)\longrightarrow 0,

(S.4b)

\displaystyle\inf_{\mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{P}_{0}}\mathds{W}_{c}(\mathbb{P}^{(k)},\mathbb{P})

\displaystyle\geq c_{\varepsilon}>0,

(S.4c)

where
c_{\varepsilon}
does not depend on
k
. Thus the value-based population separations
underlying Hotelling’s
T^{2}
and EL can become arbitrarily small along this class, while the
transport separation remains uniformly positive. For each fixed
k
, all three tests are
consistent; WP alone retains uniform separation as
k\to\infty
.

Figure S.1:
The smooth moment function in Example
S2.2
. Its
increasingly distant shallow minima make the moment values approach the null while the
required transport remains bounded away from zero.

S2.4.2
Construction for Example
S2.2

Set
\varepsilon=0.01
,
d_{0}=2
,
x_{0}=0
, and
y_{0}=-1
. For
k\geq 1
, let

\displaystyle d_{k}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{2(2^{k+1}+1)},

\displaystyle x_{k}

\displaystyle=2+2\sum_{j=1}^{k-1}d_{j}+d_{k},

\displaystyle y_{k}

\displaystyle=1+2^{-k}.

The intervals
[x_{k}-d_{k},x_{k}+d_{k}]
meet at their endpoints. Define a continuous even
piecewise linear function
f:\mathds{R}\to[-1,2]
by, for every
k\geq 0
,

\displaystyle f(x)=\begin{cases}-\dfrac{2-y_{k}}{d_{k}-\varepsilon}(x-x_{k}+\varepsilon)+y_{k},&x_{k}-d_{k}\leq x<x_{k}-\varepsilon,\\[2.84526pt]
y_{k},&x_{k}-\varepsilon\leq x<x_{k}+\varepsilon,\\[2.84526pt]
\dfrac{2-y_{k}}{d_{k}-\varepsilon}(x-x_{k}-\varepsilon)+y_{k},&x_{k}+\varepsilon\leq x\leq x_{k}+d_{k},\end{cases}

and by symmetry on the remaining negative intervals. Let

\displaystyle K_{\varepsilon}(t)

\displaystyle=C_{\varepsilon}\exp\left\{-\frac{1}{1-t^{2}/\varepsilon^{2}}\right\}\mathbbm{1}\{|t|<\varepsilon\},

\displaystyle\int_{\mathds{R}}K_{\varepsilon}(t)\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=1,

and set
h=f*K_{\varepsilon}
. Then
h
is smooth and even,
-1\leq h\leq 2
, and the constant
pieces of
f
give

\displaystyle h(x_{0})=-1,\qquad h(x_{k})=1+2^{-k},\qquad k\geq 1.

Consequently, for
\mathbb{P}^{(k)}=\frac{1}{2}\delta_{x_{0}}+\frac{1}{2}\delta_{x_{k}}
,

\mathds{E}_{\mathbb{P}^{(k)}}[h(X)]=2^{-(k+1)}
, which proves (
S.4a
).

Write
a_{k}=2^{-k}
. For any feasible
\mathbb{P}
, let
p_{0}
and
p_{k}
denote its masses at

x_{0}
and
x_{k}
. Since
h\geq-1
, feasibility implies
p_{k}\leq 1/(2+a_{k})
; hence

p_{0}p_{k}\leq p_{k}(1-p_{k})\leq(1+a_{k})/(2+a_{k})^{2}
. Equality is attained by the two-point law
with weights

\displaystyle q_{0}=\frac{1+a_{k}}{2+a_{k}},\qquad q_{k}=\frac{1}{2+a_{k}};

these weights solve
-q_{0}+(1+a_{k})q_{k}=0
. Substitution into

\frac{1}{2}\log\{(1/2)/q_{0}\}+\frac{1}{2}\log\{(1/2)/q_{k}\}
gives
(
S.4b
).

It remains to prove the uniform transport bound. Let
\mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{P}_{0}
and disintegrate any
coupling from
\mathbb{P}^{(k)}
to
\mathbb{P}
into conditional destination laws
Q_{0}
and
Q_{k}
given
the source points
x_{0}
and
x_{k}
. Since
h\geq-1
and

\frac{1}{2}\mathds{E}_{Q_{0}}[h]+\frac{1}{2}\mathds{E}_{Q_{k}}[h]=0
, we have
\mathds{E}_{Q_{k}}[h]\leq 1
.
Moreover,

\displaystyle h(y)\geq 1+a_{k}\qquad\text{whenever}\qquad\left|y-x_{k}\right|<d_{k}-\varepsilon,

because the support of the mollifier then remains inside the
k
th linear segment, where

f\geq y_{k}
. If

p_{k}=Q_{k}\{\left|Y-x_{k}\right|\geq d_{k}-\varepsilon\}
, the global bound
h\geq-1
therefore yields

\displaystyle 1\geq\mathds{E}_{Q_{k}}[h]\geq(1-p_{k})(1+a_{k})-p_{k},\qquad\text{so}\qquad p_{k}\geq\frac{a_{k}}{2+a_{k}}.

The transport cost of the mass originating at
x_{k}
is consequently at least

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}p_{k}(d_{k}-\varepsilon)^{2}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{(d_{k}-\varepsilon)^{2}}{2(2^{k+1}+1)}=\left(1-\frac{\varepsilon}{d_{k}}\right)^{2}\geq\left(1-\frac{\varepsilon}{d_{1}}\right)^{2}\triangleq c_{\varepsilon}>0.

Taking the infimum over
\mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{P}_{0}
and over couplings proves (
S.4c
).

S2.5
Exact checks for Theorem 3.1

Example S2.3

(Linear and quadratic moment functions)
.

The expansion in Theorem 3.1 agrees with the following two exactly
solvable projection problems.

(i)

If
\mathbf{h}(x)=x
, then

nR_{n}(\mathbf{h})=\left\langle\Sigma^{-1},\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{h}(X_{i})\right)^{\otimes 2}\right\rangle.

Here
\mathcal{V}_{n}=\Sigma
and
\mathcal{K}_{n}=0
, so
(6) reproduces the exact value.

(ii)

Suppose
\mathbf{h}(x)=\left\|x\right\|_{2}^{2}-1
,
\Sigma=I_{d_{X}}
,

\mathds{E}\left\|X\right\|_{2}^{2}=1
, and
\mathds{E}\left\|X\right\|_{2}^{4}<\infty
. With

\Delta_{n}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\left\|X_{i}\right\|_{2}^{2}-1),

the exact projection value satisfies

nR_{n}(\mathbf{h})=n\left(\sqrt{1+\frac{\Delta_{n}}{\sqrt{n}}}-1\right)^{2}=n\left(\frac{\Delta_{n}}{2\sqrt{n}}-\frac{\Delta_{n}^{2}}{8n}+O_{p}(n^{-3/2})\right)^{2}.

(S.5)

On the other hand, Theorem 3.1 gives

\text{RHS of (6)}=\frac{\Delta_{n}^{2}}{4}-\frac{\Delta_{n}^{3}}{8\sqrt{n}}+O_{p}(n^{-1}).

(S.6)

Expanding the exact value in (
S.5
) verifies agreement through the
stated
O_{p}(n^{-1})
remainder.

S2.6
Validity of Edgeworth expansion

The Cramér condition for
Y\in\mathds{R}^{l}
is

\sup_{\left\|t\right\|_{2}\geq b}\left|\mathds{E}\left[\exp\left(\mathrm{i}t^{\top}Y\right)\right]\right|<1\qquad\text{for every }b>0.

Precise formulation of Assumption 2.7

For one observation
X
, define the vectorized summands

\displaystyle U_{H}(X)

\displaystyle\triangleq\mathbf{h}(X),

\displaystyle U_{V}(X)

\displaystyle\triangleq\mathrm{vec}\!\left(\mathcal{D}\mathbf{h}(X)\Sigma\mathcal{D}\mathbf{h}(X)^{\top}\right),

\displaystyle U_{K}(X)

\displaystyle\triangleq\mathrm{vec}\!\left(\left(\mathcal{D}h^{\beta}(X)\Sigma\mathcal{D}^{2}h^{\gamma}(X)\Sigma\mathcal{D}h^{\omega}(X)^{\top}\right)_{\beta,\gamma,\omega\in[d_{h}]}\right),

\displaystyle U_{W}(X)

\displaystyle\triangleq\mathrm{vec}\!\left(\mathbf{h}(X)^{\otimes 2}\right),

and stack them into the finite-dimensional master vector

F_{0}(X)\triangleq\left(U_{H}(X)^{\top},U_{V}(X)^{\top},U_{K}(X)^{\top},U_{W}(X)^{\top}\right)^{\top}.

Write
F_{0}=(f_{1},\ldots,f_{k})^{\top}
. Select a maximal subcollection

\tilde{F}_{0}=(\tilde{f}_{1},\ldots,\tilde{f}_{r})^{\top}
for which

\{1,\tilde{f}_{1},\ldots,\tilde{f}_{r}\}
is linearly independent as a family of
functions on
\mathds{R}^{d_{X}}
. By maximality, there are a deterministic vector
a

and a deterministic matrix
L
such that

F_{0}(x)=a+L\tilde{F}_{0}(x)\qquad\text{for every }x\in\mathds{R}^{d_{X}}.

The full content of Assumption 2.7 is that
\mathbb{P}^{\star}
is absolutely
continuous with respect to Lebesgue measure and that the reduced joint vector

\tilde{F}_{0}(X)
satisfies

\sup_{\left\|t\right\|_{2}\geq b}\left|\mathds{E}\left[\exp\left(\mathrm{i}t^{\top}\tilde{F}_{0}(X)\right)\right]\right|<1\qquad\text{for every }b>0.

(C)

This single joint condition covers the projections generating
H_{n}
,

\mathcal{V}_{n}
,
\mathcal{K}_{n}
, and
\mathcal{W}_{n}
, as well as the smooth signed-root
statistics constructed from their empirical averages. Polynomial products
that appear only in moment and cumulant calculations are not additional
coordinates in (
C
); their integrability is
supplied by Assumptions 2.9–2.10 and, for the higher-order results, by
Assumption 3.9.

More generally, when
Y
satisfies the displayed Cramér condition,

[
5
, Theorem 20.1, Corollary 20.4]
implies that
\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(Y_{i}-\mathds{E}[Y])
admits an Edgeworth expansion up to order
n^{-\frac{s-2}{2}}
provided that
\mathds{E}\left[\left\|Y\right\|_{2}^{s}\right]<\infty
for
s\geq 3
, where
(Y_{i})_{i\in[n]}
are i.i.d. copies of
Y
. Further, for a smooth vector-valued function
G\in C^{s}\left(\mathds{R}^{l}\right)
,
[
4
, Theorem 2(b)]
asserts that

\displaystyle S_{n}=\sqrt{n}\left(G\left(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}Y_{i}\right)-G\left(\mathds{E}[Y]\right)\right)

(S.7)

also admits an Edgeworth expansion when its asymptotic covariance matrix is non-singular.

In the following development,
Y
is frequently a smooth function of
X
.
The next lemma gives a useful sufficient condition for the required Cramér
property.

Lemma S2.4

(Sufficient condition for Cramér regularity
[
3
, Lemma 1.4]
)
.

Let
X
be a random vector with values in
\mathds{R}^{d_{X}}
whose distribution has
a nonzero absolutely continuous component
\bar{\mathbb{P}}
relative to Lebesgue measure on
\mathds{R}^{d_{X}}
. Let
(f_{i})_{i\in[k]}
be Borel measurable real-valued functions on
\mathds{R}^{d_{X}}
. Assume that there is an open ball
\mathcal{B}\subset\mathds{R}^{d_{X}}
on which the density of
\bar{\mathbb{P}}
is positive almost everywhere and the functions
f_{i}
are continuously differentiable. If
\{1,f_{1},\ldots,f_{k}\}
is linearly independent on
\mathcal{B}
, then the joint distribution of
(f_{i}(X))_{i\in[k]}
satisfies the Cramér condition.

Absolute continuity by itself does not imply
(
C
) for a nonlinear transformation of
X
.
Lemma
S2.4
provides a concrete verification route: it is enough
to find a ball on which the density is positive almost everywhere and

\{1,\tilde{f}_{1},\ldots,\tilde{f}_{r}\}
is linearly independent. In that case,
for every smooth map
G
used below,

G\left(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}F_{0}\left(X_{i}\right)\right)=G\left(a+L\left(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\tilde{F}_{0}(X_{i})\right)\right).

Consequently, the Edgeworth expansion in
[
4
, Theorem 2(b)]
can be applied to
S_{n}
in (
S.7
) using the reduced vector
\tilde{F}_{0}(X)
.

S2.7
Proof of Theorem 3.1

S2.7.1
Proof roadmap

The proof proceeds in five steps, summarized in Figure
S.2
.

\displaystyle nR_{n}(\mathbf{h})=\sup_{\zeta\in\mathds{R}^{d}}\{-\zeta^{\top}H_{n}-M_{n}(\zeta)\}

Proposition
S2.5

\displaystyle\sup_{\zeta\in\mathcal{Z}_{n}}\lvert M_{n}(\zeta)-F_{n}(\zeta)\rvert\leq\widetilde{O}(n^{-1})

Proposition
S2.16

\displaystyle\mathop{\arg\sup}_{\zeta\in\mathds{R}^{d}}\{-\zeta^{\top}H_{n}-M_{n}(\zeta)\}\in\mathcal{Z}_{n}

Proposition
S2.13

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} nR_{n}(\mathbf{h})&=\sup_{\zeta\in\mathcal{Z}_{n}}\{-\zeta^{\top}H_{n}-F_{n}(\zeta)\}+\widetilde{O}(n^{-1})\\
&=\xi_{n}^{\top}\mathcal{J}_{n}\xi_{n}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\mathcal{K}_{n}+\widetilde{O}(n^{-1})\end{aligned}

Proposition
S2.18
, Theorem 3.1

Assumptions 2.5, 2.6
Condition A
Assumptions 2.4, 2.5
Condition A
Assumption 2.5
Condition A

Figure S.2:
Proof roadmap for Theorem 3.1. Proposition
S2.12

shows that
Condition A
holds with probability
1-O(n^{-1})
.

Step 1:

Dual representation.

Proposition
S2.1
gives

nR_{n}(\mathbf{h})=\sup_{\zeta\in\mathds{R}^{d_{h}}}\{-\zeta^{\top}H_{n}-M_{n}(\zeta)\},\qquad H_{n}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{h}(X_{i}).

Step 2:

High-probability bounds.

Assumption 2.9 controls the empirical quantities entering the
dual problem with probability at least
1-O(n^{-1})
.

Step 3:

Localization of the dual optimizer.

On the preceding event, the supremum may be restricted to the Euclidean ball

\mathcal{Z}_{n}
with the score-adaptive radius

r_{n}=8(\|H_{n}\|_{2}\vee n^{-1/2})/\lambda_{\min}(\mathcal{V}_{n})
:

nR_{n}(\mathbf{h})=\max_{\zeta\in\mathcal{Z}_{n}}\{-\zeta^{\top}H_{n}-M_{n}(\zeta)\}.

Step 4:

Expansion of the dual objective.

Uniformly over
\mathcal{Z}_{n}
,

M_{n}(\zeta)=F_{n}(\zeta)+\varepsilon_{n}^{M}(\zeta),\qquad\sup_{\zeta\in\mathcal{Z}_{n}}|\varepsilon_{n}^{M}(\zeta)|=\widetilde{O}(n^{-1}).

Step 5:

Expansion of the supremum.

With high probability,

\sup_{\zeta\in\mathcal{Z}_{n}}\{-\zeta^{\top}H_{n}-F_{n}(\zeta)\}=\left\langle\mathcal{V}_{n},\xi_{n}^{\otimes 2}\right\rangle+\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\left\langle\mathcal{K}_{n},\xi_{n}^{\otimes 3}\right\rangle+\widetilde{O}(n^{-1}).

S2.7.2
Step 1: dual form of the projection distance
R_{n}(\mathbf{h})

The main result of this step is presented in Proposition
S2.5
, where we provide the dual form characterization of the scaled projected distance
nR_{n}(\mathbf{h})
.

Proposition S2.5

(Rescaling of the WP function)
.

Under Assumptions 2.4 and 2.5, we have

\displaystyle nR_{n}(\mathbf{h})=\sup_{\zeta\in\mathds{R}^{d_{h}}}\{-\zeta^{\top}H_{n}-M_{n}(\zeta)\},

(Dual)

where
H_{n}\triangleq\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{h}(X_{i})
, and

\displaystyle M_{n}(\zeta)=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sup_{\Delta\in\mathds{R}^{d_{X}}}\left\{\zeta^{\top}\int_{0}^{1}\mathcal{D}\mathbf{h}\left(X_{i}+n^{-1/2}\Delta u\right)\Delta\mathrm{d}u-\left\|\Delta\right\|_{\Sigma}^{2}\right\}\qquad\forall\zeta\in\mathds{R}^{d_{h}}.

Further,
M_{n}(\cdot)
is a proper convex function taking values in
[0,\infty]
.

The proof of Proposition
S2.5
relies on the following strong duality result that formulates the value
R_{n}(\mathbf{h})
as the optimal value of an optimization problem on
\mathds{R}^{d_{h}}\times\mathds{R}^{d_{X}}
.

Lemma S2.6

(Strong duality of
R_{n}(\mathbf{h})
)
.

Under Assumptions 2.4 and 2.5, we have

\displaystyle R_{n}(\mathbf{h})=\sup_{\zeta\in\mathds{R}^{d_{h}}}\left\{-\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sup_{x\in\mathds{R}^{d_{X}}}\left\{\zeta^{\top}\mathbf{h}(x)-\left\|x-X_{i}\right\|_{\Sigma}^{2}\right\}\right\}.

(S.8)

Moreover,
R_{n}(\mathbf{h})<\infty
, and the supremum over
\zeta\in\mathds{R}^{d_{h}}
in (
S.8
) is always attainable.

Proof of Lemma
S2.6
.

By
[
7
, Proposition 3]
, we obtain (
S.8
). For the second claim, Assumption 2.4 gives

\mathbf{0}\in\conv\{\mathbf{h}(x):x\in\mathds{R}^{d_{X}}\}
. Thus, by

[
48
, Theorem 2.3]
, there exists a discrete distribution

\mathbb{P}^{\prime}=\sum_{i=1}^{s}w_{i}\delta_{X^{\prime}_{i}}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathds{R}^{d_{X}})
such that

\mathbf{0}=\mathds{E}_{\mathbb{P}^{\prime}}[\mathbf{h}(X)]
, where
\mathbb{P}^{\prime}
is supported on

(X_{i}^{\prime})_{i=1}^{s}
with corresponding weights
(w_{i})_{i=1}^{s}
. Therefore,

R_{n}(\mathbf{h})\leq\mathds{W}_{c}(\mathbb{Q}_{n},\mathbb{P}^{\prime})<\infty
. By

[
38
, Theorem 1]
, the supremum over

\zeta\in\mathds{R}^{d_{h}}
in (
S.8
) is attained.
∎

We now prove Proposition
S2.5
.

Proof of Proposition
S2.5
.

By Lemma
S2.6
, we have

\displaystyle R_{n}(\mathbf{h})

\displaystyle~=\sup_{\zeta\in\mathds{R}^{d_{h}}}\left\{-\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sup_{\Delta\in\mathds{R}^{d_{X}}}\left\{\zeta^{\top}\mathbf{h}\left(\Delta+X_{i}\right)-\left\|\Delta\right\|_{\Sigma}^{2}\right\}\right\}

\displaystyle~=\sup_{\zeta\in\mathds{R}^{d_{h}}}\left\{-\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\zeta^{\top}\mathbf{h}(X_{i})-\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sup_{\Delta\in\mathds{R}^{d_{X}}}\left\{\zeta^{\top}\left(\mathbf{h}(\Delta+X_{i})-\mathbf{h}(X_{i})\right)-\left\|\Delta\right\|_{\Sigma}^{2}\right\}\right\}

\displaystyle~=\sup_{\zeta\in\mathds{R}^{d_{h}}}\left\{-\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\zeta^{\top}\mathbf{h}(X_{i})-\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sup_{\Delta\in\mathds{R}^{d_{X}}}\left\{\zeta^{\top}\int_{0}^{1}\mathcal{D}\mathbf{h}(X_{i}+\Delta u)\Delta\mathrm{d}u-\left\|\Delta\right\|_{\Sigma}^{2}\right\}\right\}.

Multiplying both sides by
n
and applying the change of variables
\zeta^{\prime}\leftarrow\sqrt{n}\zeta
and
\Delta^{\prime}\leftarrow\sqrt{n}\Delta
, we get

\displaystyle nR_{n}(\mathbf{h})=\sup_{\zeta^{\prime}\in\mathds{R}^{d_{h}}}\left\{-\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\zeta^{\prime\top}\mathbf{h}(X_{i})-\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sup_{\Delta^{\prime}\in\mathds{R}^{d_{X}}}\left\{\zeta^{\prime\top}\int_{0}^{1}\mathcal{D}\mathbf{h}(X_{i}+n^{-\frac{1}{2}}\Delta^{\prime}u)\Delta^{\prime}\mathrm{d}u-\left\|\Delta^{\prime}\right\|_{\Sigma}^{2}\right\}\right\}.

Renaming the variables completes the first claim of the proof.

For the second claim, to establish the convexity of
M_{n}(\cdot)
, it suffices to note that

\displaystyle\sup_{\Delta\in\mathds{R}^{d_{X}}}\left\{\zeta^{\top}\int_{0}^{1}\mathcal{D}\mathbf{h}\left(X_{i}+n^{-1/2}\Delta u\right)\Delta\mathrm{d}u-\left\|\Delta\right\|_{\Sigma}^{2}\right\}

is the supremum of linear functions of
\zeta
and is therefore convex in
\zeta
. Since
M_{n}(\mathbf{0})=0
, the effective domain of
M_{n}(\cdot)
is nonempty. Also, for all
\zeta\in\mathds{R}^{d_{h}}
,

\displaystyle M_{n}(\zeta)\geq\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left\{\zeta^{\top}\int_{0}^{1}\mathcal{D}\mathbf{h}\left(X_{i}+n^{-1/2}\mathbf{0}u\right)\mathbf{0}\mathrm{d}u-\left\|\mathbf{0}\right\|_{\Sigma}^{2}\right\}=0.

Thus,
M_{n}(\cdot)
is a proper convex function, which completes the proof.
∎

S2.7.3
Step 2: bounding the tail probabilities

At this step, we establish the bounds in
Condition A
with failure probability
O(n^{-1})
, matching the error order in Theorem 3.1.

Lemma S2.7

(Tail bound of supremum)
.

Suppose that
(X_{i},1\leq i\leq n)
are independent copies of a random variable
X
with law
\mathbb{P}
and
\mathds{E}|X|^{\rho}<\infty
for some
\rho>0
. Then

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\sup_{1\leq i\leq n}|X_{i}|\geq n^{\frac{2}{\rho}}\right)\leq\frac{\mathds{E}|X|^{\rho}}{n}.

Proof of Lemma
S2.7
.

By Markov’s inequality, we find

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\sup_{1\leq i\leq n}|X_{i}|\geq n^{\frac{2}{\rho}}\right)\leq\frac{\mathds{E}\left[\sup_{1\leq i\leq n}|X_{i}|^{\rho}\right]}{n^{2}}\leq\frac{\mathds{E}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{n}|X_{i}|^{\rho}\right]}{n^{2}}=\frac{\mathds{E}|X|^{\rho}}{n}.

This observation completes the proof.
∎

Lemma S2.8

(Tail bound of average I)
.

Suppose that
(X_{i},1\leq i\leq n)
are independent copies of a random variable
X
with law
\mathbb{P}
, with
\mathds{E}[X]=0
and
\mathds{E}[|X|^{\rho}]<\infty
for
\rho\in[1,2]
. Then

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\left|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}\right|\geq n^{\frac{2-\rho}{\rho}}\right)\leq\frac{2\mathds{E}[|X|^{\rho}]}{n}.

Proof of Lemma
S2.8
.

By
[
15
, Lemma 4.2]
, we get
\mathds{E}\left[\left|\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}\right|^{\rho}\right]\leq 2n\mathds{E}\left[\left|X\right|^{\rho}\right]
for
\rho\in[1,2]
. By Markov’s inequality, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\left|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}\right|\geq g(n)\right)\leq\frac{\mathds{E}\left[\left|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}\right|^{\rho}\right]}{g(n)^{\rho}}=\frac{\mathds{E}\left[\left|\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}\right|^{\rho}\right]}{n^{\rho}g(n)^{\rho}}\leq\frac{2n\mathds{E}[|X|^{\rho}]}{n^{\rho}g(n)^{\rho}}=\frac{2\mathds{E}[|X|^{\rho}]}{n^{\rho-1}g(n)^{\rho}}.

Taking
g(n)=n^{(2-\rho)/\rho}
gives the desired result.
∎

Lemma S2.9

(Tail bound of average II)
.

Suppose that
(X_{i},i\in[n])
are independent copies of a

d_{h}
-dimensional random vector
X
with law
\mathbb{P}
, mean zero, and
covariance matrix
V=\mathrm{Cov}(X,X)\in\mathbb{S}_{++}
. If, for an integer
r\geq 1
, the
distribution of
\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}
admits an Edgeworth
expansion through terms of order
n^{-r}
with remainder
o(n^{-r})
, then
for every
a>\sqrt{2r\lambda_{\max}(V)}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\left\|\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}\right\|_{2}\geq a\sqrt{\log(n)}\right)=o\left(\frac{1}{n^{r}}\right).

Proof of Lemma
S2.9
.

By the Edgeworth expansion
[
5
, Corollary 20.4 and Lemma 7.2]
, we have

\displaystyle\sup_{B\in\mathcal{B}}\left|\mathbb{P}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}\in B\right)-\sum_{s=0}^{2r}n^{-\frac{s}{2}}\int_{v\in B}p_{s}(v)\phi_{V}(v)\mathrm{d}v\right|=o\left(\frac{1}{n^{r}}\right),

(S.9)

where
\mathcal{B}
is the set of Borel-measurable convex sets in
\mathds{R}^{d_{h}}
,
\phi_{V}(x)
is the density of a normal vector with mean zero and covariance matrix
V\in\mathds{R}^{d_{h}\times d_{h}}
, and for each
s
,
p_{s}(x)
is a polynomial of degree
3s
with coefficients depending on cumulants of
X
. Apply (
S.9
) to the convex ball
B_{n}=\{x:\left\|x\right\|_{2}<a\sqrt{\log(n)}\}
and then take complements. Since
\int p_{0}\phi_{V}=1
and
\int p_{s}\phi_{V}=0
for
s\geq 1
, we get

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\left\|\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}\right\|_{2}\geq a\sqrt{\log(n)}\right)=\sum_{s=0}^{2r}n^{-\frac{s}{2}}\int_{\left\|v\right\|_{2}\geq a\sqrt{\log(n)}}p_{s}(v)\phi_{V}(v)\mathrm{d}v+o\left(\frac{1}{n^{r}}\right).

For each
s=0,\ldots,2r
, the corresponding integral on the right-hand side is bounded by a constant times

\displaystyle\int_{\left\|v\right\|_{2}\geq a\sqrt{\log(n)}}\left\|v\right\|_{2}^{3s}\phi_{V}(v)\mathrm{d}v\leq C_{s}\{\log(n)\}^{c_{s}}\exp\left\{-\frac{a^{2}\log(n)}{2\lambda_{\max}(V)}\right\}=o\left(\frac{1}{n^{r}}\right),

(S.10)

for constants
C_{s},c_{s}<\infty
, where the last equality uses
a^{2}>2r\lambda_{\max}(V)
. The derivation of the Gaussian tail bound in (
S.10
) is deferred to Supplementary Section
S2.11
. Therefore, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\left\|\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}\right\|_{2}\geq a\sqrt{\log(n)}\right)=o\left(\frac{1}{n^{r}}\right).

This observation completes the proof.
∎

Lemma S2.10

(Matrix Bernstein inequality
[
56
, Theorem 6.6.1 and Discussion 6.6.2]
)
.

Suppose that
(A_{i},1\leq i\leq n)
are independent copies of a self-adjoint
d_{h}\times d_{h}
random matrix
A
with law
\mathbb{P}
, with

\mathds{E}[A]=0
,
\mathds{E}[A^{2}]
finite, and
\lambda_{\min}(A)\geq-L
almost surely. Then for
B_{n}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}A_{i}
and
t\geq 0
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\lambda_{\min}(B_{n})\leq-t\right)\leq d_{h}\exp\left(\frac{-t^{2}/2}{\left\|\mathds{E}B_{n}^{2}\right\|_{2}+Lt/3}\right).

Lemma S2.11

(Tail bound of the smallest eigenvalue)
.

Suppose that
(A_{i},1\leq i\leq n)
are independent copies of a
d_{h}\times d_{h}
symmetric random matrix
A
such that
A\succeq 0
almost surely,
\mathds{E}[A]\in\mathbb{S}_{++}
, and
\mathds{E}[A^{2}]
is finite. When

\displaystyle n\geq\left(\frac{2}{9}\vee\frac{8}{\lambda_{\min}(\mathds{E}[A])^{2}}\right)\left(\left\|\mathds{E}[(A-\mathds{E}[A])^{2}]\right\|_{2}+\left\|\mathds{E}[A]\right\|_{2}\right)\log(d_{h}n),

we have
\lambda_{\min}\left(n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}A_{i}\right)\geq\frac{1}{2}\lambda_{\min}(\mathds{E}[A])
with probability at least
1-n^{-1}
.

Proof of Lemma
S2.11
.

Let
B_{n}=n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(A_{i}-\mathds{E}[A]\right)
and
L=n^{-1}\left\|\mathds{E}[A]\right\|_{2}
. Thus,

\left\|\mathds{E}B_{n}^{2}\right\|_{2}=n^{-1}\left\|\mathds{E}[(A-\mathds{E}[A])^{2}]\right\|_{2}.

Define momentarily
\hat{t}=\sqrt{2(\left\|\mathds{E}B_{n}^{2}\right\|_{2}+L)\log(d_{h}n)}
and consider a concentration range
[-\hat{t},\infty)
for
\lambda_{\min}(B_{n})
. When the sample size
n
satisfies

\displaystyle n\geq\left(\frac{2}{9}\vee\frac{8}{\lambda_{\min}(\mathds{E}[A])^{2}}\right)\left(\left\|\mathds{E}[(A-\mathds{E}[A])^{2}]\right\|_{2}+\left\|\mathds{E}[A]\right\|_{2}\right)\log(d_{h}n),

we get
\hat{t}\leq\min\{3,\frac{1}{2}\lambda_{\min}(\mathds{E}[A])\}
. By Lemma
S2.10
, since
A_{i}\succeq 0
almost surely,

\displaystyle\lambda_{\min}(A_{i}-\mathds{E}[A])\geq-\left\|\mathds{E}[A]\right\|_{2}\quad\forall i\in[n],

we obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\lambda_{\min}(B_{n})\leq-\frac{1}{2}\lambda_{\min}(\mathds{E}[A])\right)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\left(\lambda_{\min}(B_{n})\leq-\hat{t}\right)

(because
\hat{t}\leq\frac{1}{2}\lambda_{\min}(\mathds{E}[A])
)

\displaystyle\leq d_{h}\exp\left(\frac{-{\hat{t}}^{2}/2}{\left\|\mathds{E}B_{n}^{2}\right\|_{2}+L\hat{t}/3}\right)

(Lemma
S2.10
)

\displaystyle\leq d_{h}\exp\left(\frac{-{\hat{t}}^{2}/2}{\left\|\mathds{E}B_{n}^{2}\right\|_{2}+L}\right)

(because
\hat{t}\leq 3
)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}

\displaystyle\text{(plug in $\hat{t}$ and $\mathds{E}B_{n}^{2}$)}.

Thus, with probability at least
1-n^{-1}
, for any
\zeta\in\mathds{R}^{d_{h}}
such that
\left\|\zeta\right\|_{2}=1
, we have

\displaystyle\zeta^{\top}\left(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}A_{i}\right)\zeta

\displaystyle=\zeta^{\top}B_{n}\zeta+\zeta^{\top}\mathds{E}[A]\zeta

(because
B_{n}=n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}A_{i}-\mathds{E}[A]
)

\displaystyle\geq\lambda_{\min}(B_{n})+\lambda_{\min}(\mathds{E}[A])

\displaystyle\geq-\hat{t}+\lambda_{\min}(\mathds{E}[A])

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{2}\lambda_{\min}(\mathds{E}[A]).

Therefore,
\lambda_{\min}\left(n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}A_{i}\right)\geq\frac{1}{2}\lambda_{\min}(\mathds{E}[A])
.
∎

Proposition S2.12

(Joint probability tail bounds)
.

Suppose that
\mathbf{h}:\mathds{R}^{d_{X}}\rightarrow\mathds{R}^{d_{h}}
satisfies
Assumptions 2.5–2.6 and that
(X_{i},1\leq i\leq n)
are independent copies
of a
d_{X}
-dimensional random vector
X
with law
\mathbb{P}^{\star}
. Let

A=\mathcal{D}\mathbf{h}(X)\Sigma\mathcal{D}\mathbf{h}(X)^{\top}
. If Assumptions 2.7–2.9 hold and

\displaystyle n\geq\left(\frac{2}{9}\vee\frac{8}{\lambda_{\min}(\mathds{E}[A])^{2}}\right)\left(\left\|\mathds{E}[(A-\mathds{E}[A])^{2}]\right\|_{2}+\left\|\mathds{E}[A]\right\|_{2}\right)\log(d_{h}n),

define

\displaystyle W

\displaystyle\triangleq\mathrm{Cov}(\mathbf{h}(X),\mathbf{h}(X)),

\displaystyle a_{H}

\displaystyle\triangleq 2\sqrt{1\vee\lambda_{\max}(W)},

\displaystyle\sigma_{\min}

\displaystyle\triangleq\lambda_{\min}(\mathds{E}[A]),

\displaystyle\bar{r}_{n}

\displaystyle\triangleq\frac{16a_{H}}{\sigma_{\min}}\sqrt{\log(n)}.

Then, with probability at least
1-O(n^{-1})
, the following inequalities hold simultaneously:

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\text{(A1)}~\lambda_{\min}\left(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathcal{D}\mathbf{h}(X_{i})\Sigma\mathcal{D}\mathbf{h}(X_{i})^{\top}\right)\geq\frac{1}{2}\lambda_{\min}\left(\mathds{E}\left[\mathcal{D}\mathbf{h}(X)\Sigma\mathcal{D}\mathbf{h}(X)^{\top}\right]\right).\\
&\text{(A2)}~\left\|\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{h}(X_{i})\right\|_{2}\leq a_{H}\sqrt{\log(n)}.\\
&\text{(A3)}~\text{(i)}~\sup_{1\leq i\leq n}\left\|\mathcal{D}\mathbf{h}(X_{i})\right\|_{2}\leq\frac{\hat{\delta}\sqrt{n}}{\left\|\Sigma\right\|_{2}\bar{r}_{n}}.\qquad\text{(ii)}~\sup_{1\leq i\leq n}\kappa_{1}(X_{i})\leq\frac{\sqrt{n}}{\left\|\Sigma\right\|_{2}\bar{r}_{n}}.\\
\end{split}

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\text{(A4)}~\text{(i)}~\left|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left\|\mathcal{D}\mathbf{h}(X_{i})\right\|_{2}^{2}\kappa_{1}(X_{i})\right|\leq 1+\mathds{E}\left[\left\|\mathcal{D}\mathbf{h}(X)\right\|_{2}^{2}\kappa_{1}(X)\right].\\
&\quad\quad~\text{(ii)}~\left|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left\|\mathcal{D}\mathbf{h}(X_{i})\right\|_{2}^{2}\kappa_{1}(X_{i})^{2}\right|\leq 1+\mathds{E}\left[\left\|\mathcal{D}\mathbf{h}(X)\right\|_{2}^{2}\kappa_{1}(X)^{2}\right].\\
&\quad\quad~\text{(iii)}~\left|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left\|\mathcal{D}\mathbf{h}(X_{i})\right\|_{2}^{3}\kappa_{2}(X_{i})\right|\leq 1+\mathds{E}\left[\left\|\mathcal{D}\mathbf{h}(X)\right\|_{2}^{3}\kappa_{2}(X)\right].\\
\end{split}

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\text{(A5)}~\text{(i)}~\left|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left\|\mathcal{D}\mathbf{h}(X_{i})\right\|_{2}^{3}\kappa_{1}(X_{i})\kappa_{2}(X_{i})\right|\leq\sqrt{n}+\mathds{E}\left[\left\|\mathcal{D}\mathbf{h}(X)\right\|_{2}^{3}\kappa_{1}(X)\kappa_{2}(X)\right].\\
&\quad\quad~\text{(ii)}~\left|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left\|\mathcal{D}\mathbf{h}(X_{i})\right\|_{2}^{2}\kappa_{1}(X_{i})^{3}\right|\leq\sqrt{n}+\mathds{E}\left[\left\|\mathcal{D}\mathbf{h}(X)\right\|_{2}^{2}\kappa_{1}(X)^{3}\right].\\
&\text{(A6)}~\left|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left\|\mathcal{D}\mathbf{h}(X_{i})\right\|_{2}^{3}\kappa_{1}(X_{i})^{2}\kappa_{2}(X_{i})\right|\leq n+\mathds{E}\left[\left\|\mathcal{D}\mathbf{h}(X)\right\|_{2}^{3}\kappa_{1}(X)^{2}\kappa_{2}(X)\right].\end{split}

(Condition A)

We introduce
Condition A
for use in the following proof.

Proof of Proposition
S2.12
.

We prove this proposition using Assumptions 2.7–2.9 together with the
preceding lemmas. Each item below is established with probability at least

1-O(n^{-1})
.

•

By Assumption 2.9,
\mathds{E}\left[\left\|\mathcal{D}\mathbf{h}(X)\right\|_{2}^{4}\right]<\infty
. Using Lemma
S2.11
, we get (A1).

•

By Assumption 2.8,
\mathds{E}[\mathbf{h}(X)]=\mathbf{0}
. By Assumption 2.9,
\mathds{E}\left[\left\|\mathbf{h}(X)\right\|_{2}^{4}\right]<\infty
. The Cramér regularity in Assumption 2.7 therefore yields an Edgeworth expansion of
\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{h}(X_{i})
up to order
n^{-1}
. Since
a_{H}^{2}>2\lambda_{\max}(W)
, Lemma
S2.9
gives (A2).

•

By Assumption 2.9,
\mathds{E}\left[\left\|\mathcal{D}\mathbf{h}(X)\right\|_{2}^{8}\right]<\infty
and
\mathds{E}\left[\kappa_{1}(X)^{8}\right]<\infty
. A union bound and Markov’s inequality give

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\max_{i\leq n}\kappa_{1}(X_{i})>\frac{\sqrt{n}}{\left\|\Sigma\right\|_{2}\bar{r}_{n}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\mathds{E}[\kappa_{1}(X)^{8}]\left\|\Sigma\right\|_{2}^{8}\bar{r}_{n}^{8}}{n^{3}},

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\max_{i\leq n}\left\|\mathcal{D}\mathbf{h}(X_{i})\right\|_{2}>\frac{\hat{\delta}\sqrt{n}}{\left\|\Sigma\right\|_{2}\bar{r}_{n}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\mathds{E}[\left\|\mathcal{D}\mathbf{h}(X)\right\|_{2}^{8}]\left\|\Sigma\right\|_{2}^{8}\bar{r}_{n}^{8}}{\hat{\delta}^{8}n^{3}}.

Because
\bar{r}_{n}^{8}=O\{\log(n)^{4}\}
, both probabilities are
O(n^{-1})
, proving (A3).

•

By Assumption 2.9,

\displaystyle\mathds{E}\left[\left\|\mathcal{D}\mathbf{h}(X)\right\|_{2}^{4}\kappa_{1}(X)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle<\infty,

\displaystyle\mathds{E}\left[\left\|\mathcal{D}\mathbf{h}(X)\right\|_{2}^{4}\kappa_{1}(X)^{4}\right]

\displaystyle<\infty,

\displaystyle\mathds{E}\left[\left\|\mathcal{D}\mathbf{h}(X)\right\|_{2}^{6}\kappa_{2}(X)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle<\infty.

Lemma
S2.8
with
\rho=2
gives (A4).

•

By Assumption 2.9,
\mathds{E}\left[\left\|\mathcal{D}\mathbf{h}(X)\right\|_{2}^{4}\kappa_{1}(X)^{\frac{4}{3}}\kappa_{2}(X)^{\frac{4}{3}}\right]<\infty,\mathds{E}\left[\left\|\mathcal{D}\mathbf{h}(X)\right\|_{2}^{\frac{8}{3}}\kappa_{1}(X)^{4}\right]<\infty
. Using Lemma
S2.8
with
\rho=\frac{4}{3}
, we get (A5).

•

By Assumption 2.9,
\mathds{E}\left[\left\|\mathcal{D}\mathbf{h}(X)\right\|_{2}^{3}\kappa_{1}(X)^{2}\kappa_{2}(X)\right]<\infty
. Using Lemma
S2.8
with
\rho=1
, we get (A6).

Since each preceding result holds with probability at least
1-O(n^{-1})
, the Bonferroni inequality shows that they hold simultaneously with probability at least
1-O(n^{-1})
.
∎

S2.7.4
Step 3: bounding the optimal dual variable

In this section, we show that, under appropriate conditions, the supremum in the dual form (
Dual
) is attained and can therefore be replaced by a maximum. Write

\displaystyle s_{n}

\displaystyle\triangleq\left\|H_{n}\right\|_{2}\vee n^{-1/2},

\displaystyle\lambda_{n}

\displaystyle\triangleq\lambda_{\min}(\mathcal{V}_{n}),

\displaystyle r_{n}

\displaystyle\triangleq\frac{8s_{n}}{\lambda_{n}},

where
\mathcal{V}_{n}=n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathcal{D}\mathbf{h}(X_{i})\Sigma\mathcal{D}\mathbf{h}(X_{i})^{\top}
. On
Condition A
and for
n\geq 2
,
\lambda_{n}>0
and
r_{n}\leq\bar{r}_{n}
. We localize to the score-adaptive ball

\mathcal{Z}_{n}\triangleq\{\zeta\in\mathds{R}^{d_{h}}:\|\zeta\|_{2}\leq r_{n}\}.

(S.11)

Consequently, without any loss of optimality, we can add the constraint
\zeta\in\mathcal{Z}_{n}
into the optimization problem (
Dual
). The following proposition asserts this result.

Proposition S2.13

(Bound of the optimal dual variable)
.

If Assumptions 2.4 and 2.5 hold and
Condition A
is satisfied, then for

n\geq 2
satisfying

\displaystyle\frac{32a_{H}B_{\Sigma}}{\sigma_{\min}^{2}}\sqrt{\frac{\log(n)}{n}}<1,

(S.12)

where

\displaystyle B_{\Sigma}\triangleq\left\|\Sigma\right\|_{2}^{2}\left(1+\mathds{E}[\left\|\mathcal{D}\mathbf{h}(X)\right\|_{2}^{2}\kappa_{1}(X)]\right),

and
a_{H},\sigma_{\min}
are defined in Proposition
S2.12
, we have

nR_{n}(\mathbf{h})=\max_{\zeta\in\mathcal{Z}_{n}}\{-\zeta^{\top}H_{n}-M_{n}(\zeta)\}.

(S.13)

Unless stated otherwise in this section,
(X_{i},1\leq i\leq n)
are independent copies of a
d_{X}
-dimensional random vector
X
with law
\mathbb{P}^{\star}
, and
\mathds{E}
denotes expectation under
\mathbb{P}^{\star}
. The phrase “under
Condition A
” means that
(X_{i},1\leq i\leq n)
and
X
satisfy the bounds listed there.

The proof of Proposition
S2.13
requires the following preliminary result.

Lemma S2.14

(Lower bound of
M_{n}(\cdot)
)
.

Under Assumption 2.5, for any
\zeta\in\mathds{R}^{d_{h}}
, we have

\displaystyle M_{n}(\zeta)\geq\frac{\left(\zeta^{\top}\left(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathcal{D}\mathbf{h}(X_{i})\Sigma\mathcal{D}\mathbf{h}(X_{i})^{\top}\right)\zeta\right)^{2}}{4\zeta^{\top}\left(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathcal{D}\mathbf{h}(X_{i})\Sigma\mathcal{D}\mathbf{h}(X_{i})^{\top}\right)\zeta+\frac{2}{\sqrt{n}}\left\|\zeta\right\|_{2}\left(\zeta^{\top}\left(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\kappa_{1}(X_{i})\mathcal{D}\mathbf{h}(X_{i})\Sigma^{2}\mathcal{D}\mathbf{h}(X_{i})^{\top}\right)\zeta\right)}.

Here the ratio is defined to be zero when its denominator vanishes (in
particular, when
\zeta=\mathbf{0}
).

Proof of Lemma
S2.14
.

If the displayed denominator vanishes, the result follows from

M_{n}(\zeta)\geq 0
. Otherwise,
\zeta\neq\mathbf{0}
, and the following
construction is well defined.
For any
c>0
, let
\Delta_{c}\triangleq c\Sigma^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathcal{D}\mathbf{h}(X_{i})^{\top}\zeta/\left\|\zeta\right\|_{2}
. Then

\displaystyle\sup_{\Delta\in\mathds{R}^{d_{X}}}\left\{\zeta^{\top}\int_{0}^{1}\mathcal{D}\mathbf{h}(X_{i}+n^{-\frac{1}{2}}\Sigma^{\frac{1}{2}}\Delta u)\Sigma^{\frac{1}{2}}\Delta\mathrm{d}u-\left\|\Delta\right\|_{2}^{2}\right\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sup_{\Delta\in\mathds{R}^{d_{X}}}\left\{\zeta^{\top}\mathcal{D}\mathbf{h}(X_{i})\Sigma^{\frac{1}{2}}\Delta-\left\|\Delta\right\|_{2}^{2}+\zeta^{\top}\int_{0}^{1}\left(\mathcal{D}\mathbf{h}(X_{i}+n^{-\frac{1}{2}}\Sigma^{\frac{1}{2}}\Delta u)-\mathcal{D}\mathbf{h}(X_{i})\right)\Sigma^{\frac{1}{2}}\Delta\mathrm{d}u\right\}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle~\zeta^{\top}\mathcal{D}\mathbf{h}(X_{i})\Sigma^{\frac{1}{2}}\Delta_{c}-\left\|\Delta_{c}\right\|_{2}^{2}+\zeta^{\top}\int_{0}^{1}\left(\mathcal{D}\mathbf{h}(X_{i}+n^{-\frac{1}{2}}\Sigma^{\frac{1}{2}}\Delta_{c}u)-\mathcal{D}\mathbf{h}(X_{i})\right)\Sigma^{\frac{1}{2}}\Delta_{c}\mathrm{d}u

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle~\zeta^{\top}\mathcal{D}\mathbf{h}(X_{i})\Sigma^{\frac{1}{2}}\Delta_{c}-\left\|\Delta_{c}\right\|_{2}^{2}-\left\|\zeta\right\|_{2}\int_{0}^{1}\left\|\mathcal{D}\mathbf{h}(X_{i}+n^{-\frac{1}{2}}\Sigma^{\frac{1}{2}}\Delta_{c}u)-\mathcal{D}\mathbf{h}(X_{i})\right\|_{2}\left\|\Sigma^{\frac{1}{2}}\Delta_{c}\right\|_{2}\mathrm{d}u

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle~\zeta^{\top}\mathcal{D}\mathbf{h}(X_{i})\Sigma^{\frac{1}{2}}\Delta_{c}-\left\|\Delta_{c}\right\|_{2}^{2}-\frac{1}{2\sqrt{n}}\left\|\zeta\right\|_{2}\kappa_{1}(X_{i})\left\|\Sigma^{\frac{1}{2}}\Delta_{c}\right\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~c\times\zeta^{\top}\mathcal{D}\mathbf{h}(X_{i})\Sigma\mathcal{D}\mathbf{h}(X_{i})^{\top}\zeta/\left\|\zeta\right\|_{2}-c^{2}\times\zeta^{\top}\mathcal{D}\mathbf{h}(X_{i})\Sigma\mathcal{D}\mathbf{h}(X_{i})^{\top}\zeta/\left\|\zeta\right\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle-c^{2}\times\frac{\left\|\zeta\right\|_{2}}{2\sqrt{n}}\kappa_{1}(X_{i})\zeta^{\top}\mathcal{D}\mathbf{h}(X_{i})\Sigma^{2}\mathcal{D}\mathbf{h}(X_{i})^{\top}\zeta/\left\|\zeta\right\|_{2}^{2},

where the first inequality follows by substituting the chosen vector
\Delta_{c}
,
the second follows from Hölder’s inequality, and the third follows from
Assumption 2.5.

Since
M_{n}(\zeta)
is the average of the left-hand side of the preceding inequality over
(X_{i},1\leq i\leq n)
, we obtain

\displaystyle M_{n}(\zeta)\geq

\displaystyle~c\times\zeta^{\top}\left(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathcal{D}\mathbf{h}(X_{i})\Sigma\mathcal{D}\mathbf{h}(X_{i})^{\top}\right)\zeta/\left\|\zeta\right\|_{2}-c^{2}\times\zeta^{\top}\left(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathcal{D}\mathbf{h}(X_{i})\Sigma\mathcal{D}\mathbf{h}(X_{i})^{\top}\right)\zeta/\left\|\zeta\right\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle-c^{2}\times\frac{\left\|\zeta\right\|_{2}}{2\sqrt{n}}\zeta^{\top}\left(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\kappa_{1}(X_{i})\mathcal{D}\mathbf{h}(X_{i})\Sigma^{2}\mathcal{D}\mathbf{h}(X_{i})^{\top}\right)\zeta/\left\|\zeta\right\|_{2}^{2}.

Maximizing the right-hand side of the inequality with respect to
c
gives

\displaystyle M_{n}(\zeta)\geq\frac{\left(\zeta^{\top}\left(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathcal{D}\mathbf{h}(X_{i})\Sigma\mathcal{D}\mathbf{h}(X_{i})^{\top}\right)\zeta\right)^{2}}{4\zeta^{\top}\left(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathcal{D}\mathbf{h}(X_{i})\Sigma\mathcal{D}\mathbf{h}(X_{i})^{\top}\right)\zeta+\frac{2}{\sqrt{n}}\left\|\zeta\right\|_{2}\left(\zeta^{\top}\left(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\kappa_{1}(X_{i})\mathcal{D}\mathbf{h}(X_{i})\Sigma^{2}\mathcal{D}\mathbf{h}(X_{i})^{\top}\right)\zeta\right)}.

This observation completes the proof.
∎

Proof of Proposition
S2.13
.

Under Assumptions 2.4 and 2.5, we can invoke Proposition
S2.5
and Lemma
S2.6
to conclude that the optimization problem

\displaystyle\max_{\zeta\in\mathds{R}^{d_{h}}}\left\{-\zeta^{\top}H_{n}-M_{n}(\zeta)\right\}

always has at least one finite optimizer.

Define

\displaystyle\widehat{B}_{n}\triangleq\left\|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\kappa_{1}(X_{i})\mathcal{D}\mathbf{h}(X_{i})\Sigma^{2}\mathcal{D}\mathbf{h}(X_{i})^{\top}\right\|_{2}.

Condition A
(A4)(i) gives
\widehat{B}_{n}\leq B_{\Sigma}
. Moreover, (A1)–(A2) give

\displaystyle\lambda_{n}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{2}\sigma_{\min},

\displaystyle s_{n}

\displaystyle\leq a_{H}\sqrt{\log(n)},

\displaystyle r_{n}

\displaystyle\leq\bar{r}_{n}.

Consequently, condition (
S.12
) implies

\displaystyle\frac{8\widehat{B}_{n}s_{n}}{\lambda_{n}^{2}\sqrt{n}}\leq\frac{32a_{H}B_{\Sigma}}{\sigma_{\min}^{2}}\sqrt{\frac{\log(n)}{n}}<1.

Now take
\zeta\notin\mathcal{Z}_{n}
and write
r=\left\|\zeta\right\|_{2}>r_{n}
. Lemma
S2.14
, monotonicity in
r
, and the definitions of
r_{n}
and
s_{n}
yield

\displaystyle\frac{M_{n}(\zeta)}{r}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\lambda_{n}^{2}r}{4\lambda_{n}+2\widehat{B}_{n}r/\sqrt{n}}\geq\frac{\lambda_{n}^{2}r_{n}}{4\lambda_{n}+2\widehat{B}_{n}r_{n}/\sqrt{n}}

\displaystyle=\frac{2s_{n}}{1+4\widehat{B}_{n}s_{n}/(\lambda_{n}^{2}\sqrt{n})}>s_{n}\geq\left\|H_{n}\right\|_{2}.

Hence
r^{-1}\{-\zeta^{\top}H_{n}-M_{n}(\zeta)\}<0
outside
\mathcal{Z}_{n}
.
Every optimizer
\zeta_{n}^{\star}
of the optimization problem
\max_{\zeta\in\mathds{R}^{d_{h}}}\left\{-\zeta^{\top}H_{n}-M_{n}(\zeta)\right\}
satisfies

\displaystyle-(\zeta_{n}^{\star})^{\top}H_{n}-M_{n}(\zeta_{n}^{\star})\geq-\mathbf{0}^{\top}H_{n}-M_{n}(\mathbf{0})=0.

Therefore,
\left\|\zeta_{n}^{\star}\right\|_{2}\leq r_{n}
, which proves the claim.
∎

S2.7.5
Step 4: expanding
M_{n}(\zeta)

In this section, we show that when
Condition A
holds,
\mathcal{Z}_{n}
defined in (
S.11
) lies inside the effective domain of
M_{n}(\cdot)
, that is,
M_{n}(\zeta)
is finite for any
\zeta\in\mathcal{Z}_{n}
. Moreover, we also show that
M_{n}(\zeta)
can be approximated by a polynomial function of
\zeta
on the set
\mathcal{Z}_{n}
.

Lemma S2.15

(
\Delta_{n,i}
inside
M_{n}
)
.

If Assumption 2.5 and
Condition A
hold, then for
\zeta\in\mathcal{Z}_{n}
and every
i\in[n]
, the optimization problem

\displaystyle\max_{\Delta\in\mathds{R}^{d_{X}}}\left\{\zeta^{\top}\int_{0}^{1}\mathcal{D}\mathbf{h}\left(X_{i}+n^{-1/2}\Delta u\right)\Delta\mathrm{d}u-\left\|\Delta\right\|_{\Sigma}^{2}\right\}

(S.14a)

has a non-empty solution set; hence
M_{n}(\zeta)<\infty
for
\zeta\in\mathcal{Z}_{n}
. Let
\Delta_{n,i}(\zeta)
be any solution to the maximization problem. We have:

(i)

\Delta_{n,i}(\zeta)
satisfies the first-order optimality condition:

\displaystyle 2\Delta_{n,i}(\zeta)

\displaystyle=\Sigma\mathcal{D}\mathbf{h}(X_{i}+n^{-\frac{1}{2}}\Delta_{n,i}(\zeta))^{\top}\zeta.

(S.14b)

(ii)

The norm of
\Delta_{n,i}(\zeta)
is bounded by

\displaystyle\left\|\Delta_{n,i}(\zeta)\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\left\|\mathcal{D}\mathbf{h}(X_{i})\right\|_{2}\left\|\Sigma\right\|_{2}\left\|\zeta\right\|_{2}.

(S.14c)

Proof of Lemma
S2.15
.

To prove the first claim, by Assumption 2.5, we have, for any
i\in[n]
,

\displaystyle\zeta^{\top}\int_{0}^{1}\mathcal{D}\mathbf{h}\left(X_{i}+n^{-1/2}\Delta u\right)\Delta\mathrm{d}u-\left\|\Delta\right\|_{\Sigma}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\zeta^{\top}\int_{0}^{1}\left(\mathcal{D}\mathbf{h}\left(X_{i}+n^{-1/2}\Delta u\right)-\mathcal{D}\mathbf{h}\left(X_{i}\right)\right)\Delta\mathrm{d}u+\zeta^{\top}\mathcal{D}\mathbf{h}\left(X_{i}\right)\Delta-\left\|\Delta\right\|_{\Sigma}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{\left\|\zeta\right\|_{2}\kappa_{1}(X_{i})}{2\sqrt{n}}\left\|\Delta\right\|_{2}^{2}+\zeta^{\top}\mathcal{D}\mathbf{h}\left(X_{i}\right)\Delta-\left\|\Delta\right\|_{\Sigma}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\Big(\frac{\left\|\zeta\right\|_{2}\kappa_{1}(X_{i})}{2\sqrt{n}}-\frac{1}{\left\|\Sigma\right\|_{2}}\Big)\left\|\Delta\right\|_{2}^{2}+\zeta^{\top}\mathcal{D}\mathbf{h}\left(X_{i}\right)\Delta.

Under
Condition A
(A3)(ii),
\left\|\zeta\right\|_{2}\leq r_{n}\leq\bar{r}_{n}
gives

\displaystyle\frac{\left\|\zeta\right\|_{2}\kappa_{1}(X_{i})}{2\sqrt{n}}-\frac{1}{\left\|\Sigma\right\|_{2}}\leq\frac{1}{2\left\|\Sigma\right\|_{2}}-\frac{1}{\left\|\Sigma\right\|_{2}}=-\frac{1}{2\left\|\Sigma\right\|_{2}}<0.

As a result, as
\|\Delta\|_{2}
goes to infinity, the objective value in (
S.14a
) goes to
-\infty
. Since the objective function is continuous with respect to
\Delta
, the maximum is attained.

To prove the second claim, since

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}\mathcal{D}\mathbf{h}\left(X_{i}+n^{-1/2}\Delta u\right)\Delta\mathrm{d}u=\sqrt{n}\left(\mathbf{h}\left(X_{i}+n^{-1/2}\Delta\right)-\mathbf{h}\left(X_{i}\right)\right),

then by setting the gradient of the objective in (
S.14a
) to be zero, we get

\displaystyle\mathbf{0}=\mathcal{D}\mathbf{h}(X_{i}+n^{-\frac{1}{2}}\Delta_{n,i}(\zeta))^{\top}\zeta-2\Sigma^{-1}\Delta_{n,i}(\zeta).

This leads to (
S.14b
).

To prove the third claim, by Assumption 2.5, we have

\displaystyle\left\|2\Delta_{n,i}(\zeta)\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle=\left\|\Sigma\mathcal{D}\mathbf{h}(X_{i}+n^{-\frac{1}{2}}\Delta_{n,i}(\zeta))^{\top}\zeta\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\left\|\Sigma\left(\mathcal{D}\mathbf{h}(X_{i}+n^{-\frac{1}{2}}\Delta_{n,i}(\zeta))-\mathcal{D}\mathbf{h}(X_{i})\right)^{\top}\zeta\right\|_{2}+\left\|\Sigma\mathcal{D}\mathbf{h}(X_{i})^{\top}\zeta\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\left\|\Sigma\right\|_{2}\left\|\zeta\right\|_{2}\kappa_{1}(X_{i})}{\sqrt{n}}\left\|\Delta_{n,i}(\zeta)\right\|_{2}+\left\|\mathcal{D}\mathbf{h}(X_{i})\right\|_{2}\left\|\Sigma\right\|_{2}\left\|\zeta\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\left\|\Delta_{n,i}(\zeta)\right\|_{2}+\left\|\mathcal{D}\mathbf{h}(X_{i})\right\|_{2}\left\|\Sigma\right\|_{2}\left\|\zeta\right\|_{2},

where the last inequality follows from
\left\|\Sigma\right\|_{2}\left\|\zeta\right\|_{2}\kappa_{1}(X_{i})/\sqrt{n}\leq 1
. Rearranging gives (
S.14c
).
∎

Proposition S2.16

(Asymptotic expansion of
M_{n}
)
.

If Assumptions 2.5, 2.6 and
Condition A
hold, then for
\zeta\in\mathcal{Z}_{n}
, we have

\displaystyle M_{n}(\zeta)=\underbrace{\frac{1}{4}\zeta^{\top}\mathcal{V}_{n}\zeta}_{\text{quadratic term}}+\underbrace{\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}L_{n}(\zeta)}_{\text{cubic term}}+\underbrace{\varepsilon_{n}^{M}(\zeta)}_{\text{error term}},

(S.15a)

where
L_{n}(\zeta)
is a cubic form of
\zeta=(\zeta^{(\beta)})_{\beta\in[d_{h}]}
defined by

\displaystyle L_{n}(\zeta)=\sum_{\beta,\gamma,\omega\in[d_{h}]}\frac{1}{8n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\mathcal{D}h^{\beta}(X_{i})\Sigma\mathcal{D}^{2}h^{\gamma}(X_{i})\Sigma\mathcal{D}h^{\omega}(X_{i})^{\top})\zeta^{(\beta)}\zeta^{(\gamma)}\zeta^{(\omega)}.

(S.15b)

Moreover, the higher-order error term
\varepsilon_{n}^{M}(\zeta)
satisfies

\displaystyle\left|\varepsilon_{n}^{M}(\zeta)\right|\leq C\frac{\log(n)^{6}}{n}\qquad\forall\zeta\in\mathcal{Z}_{n},

where the constant
C
is independent of
n
.

Remark S2.17
.

In (
S.15a
), the quadratic term is obtained by replacing
\mathcal{D}\mathbf{h}\left(X_{i}+n^{-1/2}\Delta u\right)
with
\mathcal{D}\mathbf{h}\left(X_{i}\right)
in the definition of
M_{n}(\zeta)
for every
i\in[n]
. The cubic term results from the replacement gap, whose scale is
n^{-1/2}\Delta\approx\frac{1}{2}n^{-1/2}\Sigma\mathcal{D}\mathbf{h}(X_{i})\zeta
, as in (
S.14b
).

Proof of Proposition
S2.16
.

If Assumption 2.5 and
Condition A
hold, then by Lemma
S2.15
, for any
\zeta\in\mathcal{Z}_{n}
, we have

\displaystyle M_{n}(\zeta)=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left\{\zeta^{\top}\int_{0}^{1}\mathcal{D}\mathbf{h}\left(X_{i}+n^{-1/2}\Delta_{n,i}(\zeta)u\right)\Delta_{n,i}(\zeta)\mathrm{d}u-\left\|\Delta_{n,i}(\zeta)\right\|_{\Sigma}^{2}\right\},

(S.16a)

\displaystyle\left\|\Delta_{n,i}(\zeta)\right\|_{2}\leq\left\|\mathcal{D}\mathbf{h}(X_{i})\right\|_{2}\left\|\Sigma\right\|_{2}\left\|\zeta\right\|_{2},

(S.16b)

where
\Delta_{n,i}(\zeta)
is an optimal solution to (
S.14a
). By (
S.16a
),

\displaystyle M_{n}(\zeta)-\frac{1}{4}\zeta^{\top}\mathcal{V}_{n}\zeta

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left\{\zeta^{\top}\int_{0}^{1}\mathcal{D}\mathbf{h}\left(X_{i}+n^{-\frac{1}{2}}\Delta_{n,i}(\zeta)u\right)\Delta_{n,i}(\zeta)\mathrm{d}u-\left\|\Delta_{n,i}(\zeta)\right\|_{\Sigma}^{2}-\frac{1}{4}\zeta^{\top}\mathcal{D}\mathbf{h}(X_{i})\Sigma\mathcal{D}\mathbf{h}(X_{i})^{\top}\zeta\right\}

\displaystyle\overset{\text{by}~\eqref{eq:opt_Delta2}}{=}~

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\zeta^{\top}\left\{\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{1}\mathcal{D}\mathbf{h}\left(X_{i}+n^{-\frac{1}{2}}\Delta_{n,i}(\zeta)u\right)\Sigma\mathcal{D}\mathbf{h}\left(X_{i}+n^{-\frac{1}{2}}\Delta_{n,i}(\zeta)\right)^{\top}\mathrm{d}u\right.

\displaystyle-\left.\frac{1}{4}\mathcal{D}\mathbf{h}\left(X_{i}+n^{-\frac{1}{2}}\Delta_{n,i}(\zeta)\right)\Sigma\mathcal{D}\mathbf{h}\left(X_{i}+n^{-\frac{1}{2}}\Delta_{n,i}(\zeta)\right)^{\top}-\frac{1}{4}\mathcal{D}\mathbf{h}(X_{i})\Sigma\mathcal{D}\mathbf{h}(X_{i})^{\top}\right\}\zeta.

(S.17)

Consider the scalar-valued function
F_{n,i}:\mathds{R}^{d_{X}}\rightarrow\mathds{R}
defined as

\displaystyle F_{n,i}(v)\triangleq\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{1}\zeta^{\top}\mathcal{D}\mathbf{h}\left(X_{i}+vu\right)\Sigma~\mathcal{D}\mathbf{h}\left(X_{i}+v\right)^{\top}\zeta\mathrm{d}u-\frac{1}{4}\zeta^{\top}\mathcal{D}\mathbf{h}\left(X_{i}+v\right)\Sigma\mathcal{D}\mathbf{h}\left(X_{i}+v\right)^{\top}\zeta.

(S.18)

Then (
S.17
) becomes

\displaystyle~\eqref{eq:Mn_expansion}=~~~

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(F_{n,i}\left(n^{-\frac{1}{2}}\Delta_{n,i}(\zeta)\right)-F_{n,i}(\mathbf{0})\right)

\displaystyle=~~~

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n^{\frac{3}{2}}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\int_{0}^{1}\mathcal{D}F_{n,i}\left(n^{-\frac{1}{2}}\Delta_{n,i}(\zeta)s\right)\Delta_{n,i}(\zeta)\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\overset{\text{by~\eqref{eq:opt_Delta2}}}{=}~

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2n^{\frac{3}{2}}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\int_{0}^{1}\mathcal{D}F_{n,i}\left(n^{-\frac{1}{2}}\Delta_{n,i}(\zeta)s\right)\Sigma\mathcal{D}\mathbf{h}\left(X_{i}+n^{-\frac{1}{2}}\Delta_{n,i}(\zeta)\right)^{\top}\zeta\mathrm{d}s,

(S.19)

where the second equality follows from the fundamental theorem of calculus. Writing
\zeta=(\zeta^{(1)},\ldots,\zeta^{(d_{h})})^{\top}
and defining
D^{(2)}:\mathds{R}^{d_{X}}\rightarrow\mathds{R}^{d_{X}\times d_{X}}
by

\displaystyle D^{(2)}(x)\triangleq\sum_{\beta=1}^{d_{h}}\zeta^{(\beta)}\mathcal{D}^{2}h^{\beta}(x),

we can compute

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}F_{n,i}(v)=~

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\left(\int_{0}^{1}\zeta^{\top}\mathcal{D}\mathbf{h}\left(X_{i}+v\right)\Sigma D^{(2)}\left(X_{i}+vu\right)u+\zeta^{\top}\mathcal{D}\mathbf{h}\left(X_{i}+vu\right)\Sigma D^{(2)}\left(X_{i}+v\right)\mathrm{d}u\right)

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\zeta^{\top}\mathcal{D}\mathbf{h}\left(X_{i}+v\right)\Sigma D^{(2)}\left(X_{i}+v\right).

As a consequence, we obtain

(
S.19
)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2n^{\frac{3}{2}}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(\mathcal{D}F_{n,i}(\mathbf{0})\Sigma\mathcal{D}\mathbf{h}\left(X_{i}\right)^{\top}\zeta\right)+\varepsilon_{n}^{M}(\zeta)

\displaystyle=\frac{L_{n}(\zeta)}{\sqrt{n}}+\varepsilon_{n}^{M}(\zeta)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\beta,\gamma,\omega\in[d_{h}]}\frac{1}{8n^{\frac{3}{2}}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\mathcal{D}h^{\beta}(X_{i})\Sigma\mathcal{D}^{2}h^{\gamma}(X_{i})\Sigma\mathcal{D}h^{\omega}(X_{i})^{\top})\zeta^{(\beta)}\zeta^{(\gamma)}\zeta^{(\omega)}+\varepsilon_{n}^{M}(\zeta).

We now consider the error term
\varepsilon_{n}^{M}(\zeta)
and obtain

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{n}^{M}(\zeta)=\frac{1}{2n^{\frac{3}{2}}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(\int_{0}^{1}\mathcal{D}F_{n,i}(n^{-\frac{1}{2}}\Delta_{n,i}(\zeta)s)\Sigma\mathcal{D}\mathbf{h}\left(X_{i}+n^{-\frac{1}{2}}\Delta_{n,i}(\zeta)\right)^{\top}-\mathcal{D}F_{n,i}(\mathbf{0})\Sigma\mathcal{D}\mathbf{h}\left(X_{i}\right)^{\top}\mathrm{d}s\right)\zeta.

(S.20)

First, we compute the term inside the integral:

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}F_{n,i}(n^{-\frac{1}{2}}\Delta_{n,i}(\zeta)s)\Sigma\mathcal{D}\mathbf{h}\left(X_{i}+n^{-\frac{1}{2}}\Delta_{n,i}(\zeta)\right)^{\top}-\mathcal{D}F_{n,i}(\mathbf{0})\Sigma\mathcal{D}\mathbf{h}\left(X_{i}\right)^{\top}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\mathcal{D}F_{n,i}(n^{-\frac{1}{2}}\Delta_{n,i}(\zeta)s)-\mathcal{D}F_{n,i}(\mathbf{0})\right)\Sigma\left(\mathcal{D}\mathbf{h}\left(X_{i}+n^{-\frac{1}{2}}\Delta_{n,i}(\zeta)\right)-\mathcal{D}\mathbf{h}\left(X_{i}\right)\right)^{\top}

\displaystyle+\mathcal{D}F_{n,i}(\mathbf{0})\Sigma\left(\mathcal{D}\mathbf{h}\left(X_{i}+n^{-\frac{1}{2}}\Delta_{n,i}(\zeta)\right)-\mathcal{D}\mathbf{h}\left(X_{i}\right)\right)^{\top}

\displaystyle+\left(\mathcal{D}F_{n,i}(n^{-\frac{1}{2}}\Delta_{n,i}(\zeta)s)-\mathcal{D}F_{n,i}(\mathbf{0})\right)\Sigma\mathcal{D}\mathbf{h}\left(X_{i}\right)^{\top}.

In addition, (
S.16b
) and
Condition A
(A3)(i) give

\displaystyle n^{-1/2}\left\|\Delta_{n,i}(\zeta)\right\|_{2}\leq n^{-1/2}\left\|\mathcal{D}\mathbf{h}(X_{i})\right\|_{2}\left\|\Sigma\right\|_{2}\left\|\zeta\right\|_{2}\leq\hat{\delta}\frac{\left\|\zeta\right\|_{2}}{\bar{r}_{n}}\leq\hat{\delta}.

Plugging the definition of
F_{n,i}
in (
S.18
), we therefore have, for
s\in[0,1]
,

\displaystyle\left\|\mathcal{D}F_{n,i}(\mathbf{0})\right\|_{2}\leq\frac{\left\|\Sigma\right\|_{2}}{4}\kappa_{1}(X_{i})\left\|\mathcal{D}\mathbf{h}(X_{i})\right\|_{2}\left\|\zeta\right\|_{2}^{2},

(S.21a)

\displaystyle\left\|\mathcal{D}F_{n,i}(n^{-\frac{1}{2}}\Delta_{n,i}(\zeta)s)-\mathcal{D}F_{n,i}(\mathbf{0})\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\left(\kappa_{1}(X_{i})\kappa_{2}(X_{i})\left\|n^{-\frac{1}{2}}\Delta_{n,i}(\zeta)\right\|_{2}^{2}+\kappa_{2}(X_{i})\left\|\mathcal{D}\mathbf{h}(X_{i})\right\|_{2}\left\|n^{-\frac{1}{2}}\Delta_{n,i}(\zeta)\right\|_{2}\right.

\displaystyle\left.+\kappa_{1}(X_{i})^{2}\left\|n^{-\frac{1}{2}}\Delta_{n,i}(\zeta)\right\|_{2}\right)\frac{5\left\|\Sigma\right\|_{2}\left\|\zeta\right\|_{2}^{2}}{4},

(S.21b)

due to Assumptions 2.5 and 2.6 (the detailed algebra is deferred to Supplementary Section
S2.11
).

Thus, by (
S.16b
),

\displaystyle\left\|\mathcal{D}F_{n,i}(n^{-\frac{1}{2}}\Delta_{n,i}(\zeta)s)-\mathcal{D}F_{n,i}(\mathbf{0})\right\|_{2}\leq

\displaystyle C\left(n^{-1}\kappa_{1}(X_{i})\kappa_{2}(X_{i})\left\|\mathcal{D}\mathbf{h}(X_{i})\right\|_{2}^{2}\left\|\zeta\right\|_{2}^{4}\right.

\displaystyle+n^{-\frac{1}{2}}\kappa_{2}(X_{i})\left\|\mathcal{D}\mathbf{h}(X_{i})\right\|_{2}^{2}\left\|\zeta\right\|_{2}^{3}

\displaystyle\left.+n^{-\frac{1}{2}}\kappa_{1}(X_{i})^{2}\left\|\mathcal{D}\mathbf{h}(X_{i})\right\|_{2}\left\|\zeta\right\|_{2}^{3}\right),

(S.21c)

where
C
is a constant dependent on
\left\|\Sigma\right\|_{2}
. We also have

\displaystyle\left\|\mathcal{D}\mathbf{h}\left(X_{i}+n^{-\frac{1}{2}}\Delta_{n,i}(\zeta)\right)-\mathcal{D}\mathbf{h}\left(X_{i}\right)\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\kappa_{1}(X_{i})\left\|n^{-\frac{1}{2}}\Delta_{n,i}(\zeta)\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq n^{-\frac{1}{2}}\left\|\Sigma\right\|_{2}\kappa_{1}(X_{i})\left\|\mathcal{D}\mathbf{h}(X_{i})\right\|_{2}\left\|\zeta\right\|_{2}\hskip 16.38895pt\text{by}~\eqref{eq:Deltaleq}.

(S.21d)

Combining (
S.21a
), (
S.21c
), and (
S.21d
) and applying
the resulting bounds to (
S.20
) gives

\displaystyle\left|\varepsilon_{n}^{M}(\zeta)\right|\leq

\displaystyle~\frac{C^{\prime}}{2n^{\frac{3}{2}}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(n^{-\frac{1}{2}}\times\left(\left\|\mathcal{D}\mathbf{h}(X_{i})\right\|_{2}^{2}\kappa_{1}(X_{i})^{2}\left\|\zeta\right\|_{2}^{4}+\left\|\mathcal{D}\mathbf{h}(X_{i})\right\|_{2}^{3}\kappa_{2}(X_{i})\left\|\zeta\right\|_{2}^{4}\right)\right.
